#10718. Preventing API Contract Drift in TypeScript Applications

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A frontend application can compile successfully and still fail when it communicates with the backend.

Imagine a backend developer changes an API response from:

The backend works correctly. The frontend also builds without errors. However, a component expecting user.name now receives undefined.

This is an example of API contract drift: the frontend and backend no longer agree on the structure or behavior of an API.

The problem becomes more difficult when several developers or teams work on different parts of an application. A change made by one team may affect another team's assumptions without producing an obvious error during development.

TypeScript helps catch many programming mistakes, but its types do not automatically validate data received over HTTP. A type declaration can describe what the application expects without proving that the server actually returns it.

In this tutorial, we'll look at a practical way to make API contracts more reliable.

Consider a typical frontend API function:

The function promises to return a User, so TypeScript allows the application to access properties such as name and email.

The return value of response.json() is not automatically checked against the User interface. The interface only provides compile-time information; it does not validate the actual JSON received from the server.

the frontend may still treat the response as a User even though the data has a different structure.

This is particularly important when working with:

Independently deployed frontend and backend applications.

APIs maintained by different engineering teams.

The solution is to validate data at the boundary where it enters the application.

Zod is a TypeScript-first schema validation library. It allows developers to describe expected data structures and validate actual values against those definitions.

Now create a schema for the user response:

First, UserSchema describes the structure that the application expects at runtime.

Second, z.infer generates the corresponding TypeScript type from the schema.

This means developers do not need to maintain a separate interface and validation definition for the same data structure.

The response is initially treated as unknown, which prevents the application from assuming that untrusted data already has the expected structure.

UserSchema.parse() validates the actual response.

If the backend returns an unexpected property type or misses a required field, Zod throws a validation error instead of allowing invalid data to move silently through the application.

You can also use safeParse() when you prefer to handle validation errors without exceptions:

This approach is useful when API responses are external or when a frontend must provide more controlled error handling.

One common mistake is placing API validation logic directly inside React components.

For a small application, this may seem convenient. As the project grows, however, it becomes difficult to reuse validation rules and identify where data transformations happen.

A better approach is to separate API schemas, service functions, and UI components.

The responsibilities are straightforward:

schemas/user.ts defines the expected user data.

api/client.ts handles common HTTP configuration.

api/users.ts contains user-related API requests.

hooks/useUser.ts manages data fetching for React.

components/UserProfile.tsx displays the validated data.

This structure makes it easier to change an API implementation without rewriting the UI.

It also makes testing more manageable because validation logic can be tested independently of React rendering.

For more ideas on organizing application code, see SitePoint's guide to modern JavaScript modules.

Runtime validation protects an application when data arrives. However, teams also need a shared description of what an API is supposed to accept and return.

OpenAPI provides a standard format for describing HTTP APIs, including:

For example, an endpoint can be described using an OpenAPI document:

This document describes what the endpoint should return.

Both frontend and backend developers can refer to the same contract instead of relying on separate assumptions or outdated documentation.

Swagger Editor helps developers write and validate OpenAPI documents. It also provides a convenient way to inspect API definitions.

Swagger UI turns an OpenAPI document into interactive API documentation, allowing developers to explore endpoints and test requests.

Redoc provides another way to generate readable API documentation from an OpenAPI specification.

These tools are particularly useful when APIs are maintained by multiple developers or consumed by several applications.

Maintaining API types manually across frontend and backend repositories creates opportunities for inconsistencies.

For example, a backend developer might add a new optional property while the frontend continues using an older interface.

One way to reduce this duplication is to generate TypeScript definitions and API clients from an OpenAPI specification.

Orval is a useful tool for this workflow.

It can generate TypeScript clients and types from an OpenAPI document, helping frontend developers work with consistent endpoint definitions.

A typical configuration might look like this:

The exact configuration depends on the Orval version and the structure of the OpenAPI document.

Once configured, the client can be regenerated when the API specification changes.

This reduces the amount of repetitive code developers need to maintain manually.

However, generated types should not be confused with runtime validation. They improve consistency during development, but an application may still need to validate responses received from external systems or services that cannot be fully trusted.

Frontend developers often need to build or test features before the corresponding backend endpoint is ready.

Waiting for a complete backend implementation can slow down development, particularly when multiple teams work independently.

Mock Service Worker (MSW) helps solve this problem by intercepting network requests and returning controlled responses.

You can define a mock handler for the user endpoint:

The frontend can now make normal HTTP requests while MSW supplies predictable responses during development or testing.

You can also simulate an invalid response:

This makes it possible to check whether the application's validation and error-handling logic behave as expected.

MSW is particularly valuable because it allows frontend tests to run without requiring a live backend environment.

Runtime validation and generated clients improve reliability, but they do not automatically guarantee that independently developed services remain compatible.

For systems with multiple services, contract testing can provide another layer of protection.

Pact is a contract-testing tool that allows consumers and providers to verify their expectations about API interactions.

Consider a frontend application that expects the user endpoint to return:

The consumer defines the expected interaction.

The backend provider can then run verification against that contract to check whether its actual response satisfies the consumer's expectations.

This helps identify breaking changes before they reach production.

These tools solve different problems. A project does not necessarily need all of them, but larger applications can benefit from combining the approaches that match their architecture.

A contract is only useful if developers consistently check it when making changes.

A practical approach is to run validation and tests automatically whenever someone opens a pull request.

For example, a GitHub Actions workflow might look like this:

The api:validate script should be configured to validate the project's OpenAPI document or run the relevant contract checks.

The test command should execute the application's automated tests.

For a production project, you may also want to include:

The objective is to detect incompatible changes before they are merged.

A failed pipeline gives developers an opportunity to investigate the change while the context is still fresh.

API contracts become harder to maintain when several teams contribute to the same system.

For example, a company might have separate teams responsible for:

If nobody owns an API contract, small changes can create unexpected problems for other teams.

Assigning clear ownership helps establish who reviews changes, maintains documentation, and coordinates breaking updates.

This becomes particularly important when an organization expands its engineering capacity across locations or adds several specialized teams. Companies planning that kind of growth may also need to consider enterprise IT recruitment as part of their wider engineering workforce strategy.

Regardless of how a team is assembled, its development process should establish clear responsibilities for API design, versioning, testing, and release coordination.

A few practical conventions can make a significant difference:

Assign an owner to every important API or service.

Document breaking changes before implementation.

Require contract-related tests in pull requests.

Keep generated clients synchronized with the API specification.

Communicate deprecations before removing existing fields.

Monitor production errors after deploying API changes.

These practices help prevent a growing engineering organization from becoming a collection of disconnected services.

API contract drift is not simply a TypeScript problem. It is a coordination problem that becomes visible when applications and services develop different expectations about the same data.

TypeScript provides useful compile-time checks, but it cannot guarantee that an HTTP response matches a declared interface.

A more reliable approach combines several techniques:

Use OpenAPI to document API expectations.

Generate TypeScript clients with tools such as Orval.

Use MSW to test frontend behavior without a live backend.

Use Pact when consumer-provider contract verification is appropriate.

Run validation and tests automatically in CI/CD.

You do not need to introduce every tool at once. Start by validating external API responses, document important endpoints, and gradually automate contract checks as the application grows.

The result is a codebase where developers can make changes with greater confidence and where frontend and backend teams have a shared understanding of how their systems communicate.

#10717. 94% of the world’s biggest songs have one surprising thing in common

While there is still no recipe for becoming a globally recognized musician, there is one thing that is consistently getting artists on the charts: TikTok.

TikTok’s recent 2026 Music Impact Report found that 94% of songs that entered the Billboard top 200 chart this year went viral on TikTok first. The link is stronger than before: in 2024, TikTok found that 84% of songs on the charts had gone viral on the platform first,  up ten percentage points in two years.

TikTok virality also affects how long a song stays on the chart. The report also found that tracks that went viral on the platform stayed on the charts for four times longer than those that didn’t catch a moment of popularity on the platform. For instance, songs that had a viral moment on TikTok would spend eight weeks on Billboard charts, as opposed to just two weeks for non-viral hits.

But the charts are not the only metric artists use to measure their success and growth, their performance on streaming platforms matters too. The report also found that TikTok virality influences streaming, with 74% of the fastest-growing artists on streaming platforms saw their streams rise alongside their views on TikTok.

Sienna Spiro is among the breakthrough artists who rose to fame through the platform, starting her career singing songs to a small audience on TikTok, eventually landing on the VMA stage this year singing her hit song Great Expectations.

While a relatively small artist just a year ago, she found a viral moment in her song Die on This Hill, with millions of fans using the sound for their TikToks, giving her recognition beyond the platform. 

Additionally, the platform’s new “add to music app” feature, which lets users connect their TikTok to their streaming service to automatically save songs from videos to their playlists, contributed to Spiro’s streaming success. According to the report, millions of users used the feature to save the single to their music apps. Spiro’s viral hit led her to enter the top 20 of the Billboard Hot 100, where it stayed for 13 weeks.

#10715. Clicks Communicator hits a supply chain snag, and it isn’t because of RAM

Clicks is still on track to release the phone with a physical keyboard within the next few months, but a Communicator launch before the end of this year isn’t looking very likely as shipping gets pushed back.

The Communicator is a fresh take on what an Android phone can be. It was first introduced at CES earlier this year, with the main selling angle being the physically integrated keyboard. Clicks wants to target those who are looking for a second device, and the Communicator fits the bill.

Last we heard, the Clicks Communicator was on track to launch and ship in December, following a cheery update detailing an upgrade to 12GB of RAM and other changes. However, Clicks posted a new update today stating that the timeline has shifted out a month because of supply chain issues caused by something other than RAM.

Unfortunately, we’ve encountered a delay with securing a critical supply chain component needed to fabricate our printed circuit board ahead of manufacturing and final assembly. Assembly will now begin in early January, with deliveries expected to start in late January.

It notes that the “critical supply chain component” is the essential fabric used for the company’s printed circuit boards. The blog post dives into some neat details about the component holding things up. The PCBs Clicks is using are made up of more material layers than some competitor boards. But the fabric used to layer those boards is in short supply at this sort of demand, with four manufacturers reportedly unable to meet the company’s required volume.

The post also spells out difficulties caused by troubles in the Middle East, and rising memory costs straining the chain of supply further, even if memory – surprisingly – isn’t the cause of this small delay. Those compounded issues have pushed production back, and users are now told to expect their Clicks Communicator pre-orders to be shipped in late January, at the earliest. Many pre-orders will likely be shipped after that because of further time constraints before Chinese New Year.

In an act of good faith, Clicks says it’s including a set of 3.5mm wired headphones in each box for orders placed prior to today.

A push from December to January isn’t massive, and Clicks still seems to be making solid time on developing a form factor we don’t often see. You can still pre-order the Clicks Communicator starting at $649.

#10714. Why your HSA needs a succession plan

Health savings accounts are usually discussed in the context of their trifecta of tax benefits: pretax money going in, tax-free compounding while the funds are inside the account, and tax-free withdrawals for qualified healthcare expenditures.

But HSAs have their limits. The HSA’s tax benefits effectively cease upon the account owner’s death, or the death of his or her spouse if that person is the beneficiary.

That makes it crucial to have a succession plan for your HSA: a plan to encourage tax-free spending during your lifetime (and your spouse’s, if you have one), as well as naming the correct beneficiary for the account to maximize the tax benefits.

HSAs give during your lifetime . . .

Whereas tax-sheltered retirement account owners will pay taxes on the dollars they put into a retirement account (Roth) or on their withdrawals (traditional tax-deferred), HSAs are a unicorn account type—the funds are tax-free every step of the way, provided you spend the money on qualified healthcare expenses.

Those tax benefits are magnified for people who pay their healthcare expenses out of pocket, invest in long-term investment assets, and are in higher tax brackets.

Given those prodigious tax benefits, an HSA investor would have a bigger balance than a person investing in an IRA, 401(k), or taxable brokerage account, assuming the same contribution amounts, rates of return, tax rates, and holding periods. That’s because investors in those other account types pay taxes along the way: at time of contribution (Roth), during accumulation (taxable), and/or at time of withdrawal (taxable, traditional tax-deferred).

. . . And take after death

If your spouse is the designated beneficiary of your HSA, the HSA becomes theirs after your death, and the tax benefits continue. They can continue to take tax-free withdrawals for qualified healthcare expenses.

But if a person other than your spouse inherits the HSA, the account becomes less attractive than the other key account types mentioned before. An HSA inherited by a nonspouse beneficiary effectively ceases to be an HSA upon the account owner’s death, and all of its tax benefits go away at that time. Moreover, the nonspouse beneficiary will need to include the inherited HSA amount in their income and pay taxes on it.

The tax treatment of an HSA inherited by a nonspouse beneficiary is a bit worse than the tax treatment of other major account types that nonspouse beneficiaries might inherit. Heirs enjoy tax-free withdrawals on inherited Roth accounts. Traditional IRA beneficiaries owe taxes, but they at least enjoy some flexibility on the timing of their withdrawals and tax bills. Heirs inheriting taxable accounts have it even better, in that their cost basis becomes whatever the account holdings’ values were on the date of your death.

You need a plan for handling your HSA

That meh tax treatment on HSAs inherited by nonspouse beneficiaries highlights the value of having a plan to maximize your HSA’s long-term tax benefits.

Step 1: Get a system for spending from it

Spending from the HSA during your and your spouse’s lifetimes is key, so every HSA owner needs a spending plan. That’s true whether you’re paying healthcare expenses as you incur them or investing the funds for retirement.

If you’ve been paying your healthcare costs out of pocket in an effort to allow your account to grow, you can later use the HSA for non-healthcare expenses, provided you have receipts to substantiate those previous out-of-pocket healthcare costs. Ideally, you’d scan and document those expenditures as you incur them. If you’re a married person who’s overseeing your household’s HSA, make sure your spouse understands the withdrawal rules, where to find receipts, and how to submit them to the HSA provider.

Step 2: Assign the correct beneficiaries

Next, give care to naming beneficiaries. If you’re married, your spouse is a sensible beneficiary for the HSA; as noted earlier, they will be able to take over ownership of the account after your death and keep its full tax benefits during their lifetime.

Nonspouse beneficiaries won’t receive the same favorable tax treatment. However, if you think a nonspouse beneficiary still will benefit from the money, that’s probably a good enough reason to leave them the HSA. But other accounts should take precedence.

A charity can also make an excellent HSA beneficiary, as the charity won’t need to pay taxes on that amount. That strategy can be particularly appropriate for single people who are charitably inclined, or for surviving spouses. If you’re married, consider making your spouse the primary beneficiary and a charity the secondary.

Step 3: Make sure your beneficiaries understand the rules about HSAs

If your spouse will inherit your HSA, be sure to underscore the value of liquidating the account during his or her lifetime.

While it’s generally not advisable for a nonspouse beneficiary to inherit an HSA, if that’s your plan, it’s important to let your loved ones know that they can reduce the taxable amount in the HSA by paying any of your unpaid medical bills from the HSA. They just need to pay those bills out of your HSA within a year of your death.

This article was provided to The Associated Press by Morningstar. For more retirement content, go to morningstar.com/retirement.

Christine Benz is director of personal finance and retirement planning for Morningstar and cohost of The Long View podcast. Subscribe to her free newsletter, Improving Your Finances.

#10710. Google retiring Nest app and Google Assistant on speakers, displays in 2027

Following the Android deprecation in September, Google Assistant is going away on speakers and smart displays next year. Additionally, Google Home is fully replacing the Nest app in January.

The Nest app on Android, iOS, and the web will stop working on January 25, 2027. The Google Home app, which requires a Google Account, will be needed to “continue controlling and receiving notifications from your Nest devices remotely.”

Officially, Google wants to “focus all of [its] engineering, security, and feature investments on one unified, modern experience.” It reiterated benefits like:

With home.nest.com going away, you have to download any custom video clips since they will not transfer over to Google Home. A one-click “Download All” button is available.

Terrible news. For all my first-gen Nest cameras (wired hello doorbell, wired outdoor cams, and multiple indoor cams), I vastly prefer the Nest app to the terrible Google Home app.

Otherwise, Nest app users do not need to do anything else, with devices automatically transferring over to the Google Home app. No Nest thermostats, cameras, doorbells, smoke alarms, or locks are losing support with this change.

Additionally, Nest Aware (1st Gen) subscriptions will automatically switch to Google Home Premium “with no additional cost and no credit card re-entry required at this time.”

The other change announced today is that Google Assistant on speakers and smart displays is going away on August 2, 2027. It will be replaced by the Gemini for Home voice assistant. Google notes that natural language support replaces “rigid, static and highly specific voice commands.” Additionally:

You can upgrade to Gemini today, while Google is expanding international availability over the coming months. Google will share more updates closer to the deadline.

#10709. Why your boss can basically fire you for anything—and why it’s not like that outside the U.S.

We’ve all seen the scene play out in media: A character stands up at work, dramatically declares they quit, and walks out of the office for the last time. While many people will surely empathize with that desire, the reason why a sudden resignation is possible in the U.S. isn’t as great as such a display makes it out to be. 

Mostly because it goes both ways: an employer can axe you at the drop of a hat, in theory—even without a warning, PIP, or other chance to improve.

The U.S. has something called at-will employment, which means that you can leave—or lose—your job without giving any notice.

A job that’s at-will “can end at any moment, for good reason, bad reason, or no reason at all, and with no procedural mandates,” says Gali Racabi, an assistant professor at Cornell’s School of Industrial and Labor Relations and an associated faculty member of Cornell Law School. 

Currently, at-will employment is the law of the land in Washington, D.C., and every U.S. state except Montana. 

“This makes work in the US really easy to sever,” Racabi says.

He adds: “The at-will rule is a travesty, and a mark of moral flaw in our economic order. We need to do away with it ASAP.” 

Critiques of at-will employment

Despite being the default in America—a country whose culture is associated with entrepreneurialism, independence and one that favors employers without much safety-net protection for workers—at-will employment causes a number of concerns. 

Most prominent, perhaps, is a person suddenly losing their income and the many repercussions that can come with that. The practice is also especially risky in the U.S., where individuals often rely on their job for necessary benefits like healthcare—something many other nations provide outside of employment, Racabi says. Say, for instance, a person has an operation scheduled for next week and gets laid off today. Suddenly, they don’t have coverage for a necessary procedure. 

That’s a more extreme example. But even losing health insurance without a big medical issue coming up, and no time to find an alternative, can have devastating repercussions around the cost of medicine, seeing a doctor, or having security in an emergency. 

At-will employment can also complicate the process of proving retaliation or discrimination as the reason for a layoff. 

“Having the at-will rule also makes it really difficult to enforce other work-related laws, like anti-discrimination law,” says Racabi. “If an employer can simply terminate someone without notice, reason, or hearing, it makes proving discriminatory intent extremely hard.” The employer doesn’t have to prove any other justification for the firing.

In a 2022 survey from the National Employment Law Project, 69% of U.S. workers who had experienced a layoff or firing reported that they were either given no or an unfair reason. On top of that, 72% of these participants reported being terminated without a chance to improve or any warning. Overall, 66% of workers surveyed said they support the enactment of just-cause policies. 

Of course, not everyone thinks at-will employment should be thrown out. Carson Young, an associate professor at West Virginia University’s John Chambers College of Business and Economics, argues that at-will employment is “complicated.” It can allow workers to earn higher salaries and have more flexibility than with a just cause system, since the setup potentially lowers employer costs around hiring extra people, with less incentive to keep anyone onboard. 

Young also believes that at-will employment grants workers more freedom to leave a job as they please. He also argues that it could also make employers more likely to hire new employees, as they don’t have to make as big of a commitment to the employee if it ends up not being a good fit, a project ends, or the thing they were hired for doesn’t work out. 

“Employers in [countries] with more job protections are thus more reluctant to hire, because they face greater costs if the hire doesn’t work out,” Young says. 

How the U.S. compares to other countries

The U.S. is fairly unique in having at-will employment, with many other developed countries requiring just cause and notice to terminate an employee. In turn, the individual is often also required to provide notice when leaving a position.

“U.S. courts tend to favor freedom of contract and employer-employee flexibility over job security,” says Young. But “employment law in most other countries places a greater emphasis on job security over freedom of contract and flexibility.”

In the U.K., arguably the country most directly compared to the U.S., an employer can only let you go for “a valid reason that they can justify” and if they’ve “acted reasonably in the circumstance.” An employer will also typically have to give notice of a layoff between one to 12 weeks prior to termination, depending on the length of employment. An employee must give at least a week’s notice, but it can be longer depending on their contract. 

Yet, the U.K. also has zero-hour contracts—which mean an employer doesn’t have to guarantee any work and the individual, in turn, doesn’t have to accept any. These casual contracts are common in positions such as retail or hospitality. 

Critics of the U.K.’s zero-hour contracts point to issues similar to that of at-will employment: Workers on zero-hour contracts “will find it difficult to plan their ordinary lives and pay their bills because they are guaranteed no shifts and will find they have no regular or fixed income stream,” says David Cabrelli, a professor of labor law at the University of Edinburgh’s Law School. 

“Seen from the perspective of job and earnings security, both employment-at-will and zero-hours contracts put workers in a very vulnerable and precarious position.”

Could the U.S. get rid of at-will employment?

The U.K.’s Employment Rights Act 2025 has set new regulations for zero-hour contracts, including a right to guaranteed hours and pay for last-minute shift cancellations. The changes have not yet rolled out, with some employers and unions criticizing the new measures, the Guardian reports. 

Generally, the U.S. is not typically seen as a country that strongly values workers’ rights, instead valuing the good of the employer or business. But could the U.S. also pursue changes? 

Some caveats already exist in circumstances such as whistle-blowing or alleging sexual harassment. “However, these exceptions are issue-specific and the legal position could be improved for workers by the introduction of a just cause dismissal regime,” says Cabrelli. 

So, then, could just cause ever become the law of the land in the U.S.—a place where workers’ rights are rarely prioritized? 

“At-will is law, and we can change laws,” says Racabi. “We can enact statutory just-cause or procedural mandate laws at the federal, state, and local levels. We can do those without significant legal pushback. 

Racabi continues: “We can also support unionization; that one of its byproducts is increased workers’ protection via collective bargaining agreements.” 

At-will employment might be the norm in the U.S., but that doesn’t mean it’s the best option. Or that it has to be around forever.

As we're tracking the best SSD deals in real-time leading up to Black Friday, we occasionally come across a drive that's too good to leave for our general roundup. Such is the case here with the Team Group T-Force G50 Evo in its 4TB capacity, which is on sale for $399.99 ahead of the weekend. At just $0.10 per GB, it's easily one of the best deals on a high-capacity SSD we've seen in months.

SSDs are expensive across the board right now, but they're especially expensive if you're after a high-capacity model. Although we have seen more reasonable pricing out of 1TB and 2TB models, 4TB and 8TB SSDs range anywhere from $500 to well over $1,000 (some are even listed as high as $2,000).

At just $0.10 per GB, this 4TB T-Force drive clocks in at the same price as many 1TB and 2TB models. For context, the next cheapest 4TB drive we could find is $0.12 / GB, and many reach above $0.15 per GB.

This T-Force G50 Evo offers sequential read speeds of 5,000 MB/s and write speeds of up to 4,800 MB/s.

This is an entry-level PCIe 4.0 drive that advertises sequential read speeds of up to 5,000 MB/s and sequential write speeds of up to 4,700 MB/s. It includes SLC caching, and although it isn't the fastest PCIe 4.0 drive, it more than makes up for speed in its price and capacity.

In many instances, such as gaming, you're unlikely to notice much of a difference compared to a pricier PCIe 5.0 drive. Even at these speeds, the T-Force G50 is more than capable of complex modern streaming systems, as well as Microsoft's DirectStorage.

If you're trying to pick up an SSD as a cache for your video editor, or a similarly latency-intensive scenario, a PCIe 5.0 drive is still the way to go. You can find sales on those drive in our SSD price index.

For maximum capacity, however, don't miss the T-Force G50 Evo 4TB at $399.99.

#10705. Utah asks to drop criminal charges against Kalshi and its founders

Utah asked a court to dismiss the criminal charges it filed last month against prediction market operator Kalshi and two of its executives, citing Kalshi’s decision to halt trading in the state that had accused it of running an illegal gambling enterprise.

The one-sentence motion filed Tuesday in a Salt Lake City-based court by state Attorney General Derek Brown referenced a “change in circumstance.” Brown’s office noted that it could refile the case if Kalshi resumes offering trades on its platform there.

“Kalshi has pulled out of Utah, and if it returns, so will the charges,” Brown said in a statement.

Utah has been on the front lines of a battle between states and the federal government over who regulates the markets. Litigation has spread across more than 20 states, creating a fragmented legal landscape that analysts say will likely be settled by the U.S. Supreme Court.

The felony charges filed by Utah include 13 counts of violating the state’s prohibition on gambling and another charge involving profiting off illegal activity.

The state’s charging documents name Kalshi and its cofounders, chief executive officer Tarek Mansour and chief operating officer Luana Lopes Lara. They are due in court in Salt Lake City on December 4, unless a judge agrees to dismiss the case.

Utah law prohibits gambling, and Utah considers prediction markets gambling, Brown’s office said. Many other states contend that the platforms’ sports trading activities are flouting their laws that regulate sports betting and casino gambling.

Utah’s felony charges represented a new tactic in how states are trying to assert regulatory authority over the fast-expanding activity. Brown’s office also has asked a court to make Kalshi give up the profits it made in Utah.

Kalshi, the nation’s largest prediction market operator, said Monday it had halted trading in Utah after it became clear that a federal judge hearing a case brought by the U.S. Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) wouldn’t immediately stop Utah’s attempts to shut it down there.

A Kalshi spokeswoman called it an “unfortunate outcome for the more than 177,000 Utahns who use Kalshi” but said the company will continue to fight in court for the right to operate in Utah.

Kalshi, like the CFTC, argues that prediction markets offer “event contracts” that should be considered swaps under a federal law that grants exclusive regulation to the federal agency. Utah and at least 44 other states have opposed that view.

Kalshi sued Utah in February, asking a federal judge to prevent the state from enforcing its gambling restrictions on the platform. A federal judge rejected the request, and an appeal is pending.

Trading activity on Kalshi is halted in Nevada and partially in Michigan and Washington, according to Kalshi.

—By Marc Levy, Associated Press

#10703. 10 Best AI App Builders I've Used in 2026 (and What Each One Gets Wrong)

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI App Builders are exploding in popularity, and for good reason. It’s never been easier to build real, functional apps and internal tools using app builders. To test them all, I built the same app on 10 of the most popular AI app builders and tested each across usability, cost, correctness, and ease of use. Most of them can turn a paragraph of description into something that looks like software within a single session. The differences show up around the tenth prompt, when you learn what each edit costs and who has to fix the code once something breaks. I took every price from the vendor's own pricing page on October 29, 2026.

TLDR: Of the best AI app builders I've used, Softr is the one I'd hand to a team that needs auth, data, user groups or integrations. The other nine I tested include code generators such as Lovable and Bolt and visual platforms such as Bubble and Glide, and each one carries a cost or upkeep trade-off worth seeing before you commit.

I looked at the same seven things in every tool, from the first prompt onward:

Softr gets used a lot by operations and business teams as their AI app builder for secure client portals, internal tools and other business apps, none of which require code. Write a short brief in your own words, and the AI Co-Builder turns it into a database plus the core app that sits on it: tables, pages and layouts, the data connections behind them, plus user groups and permissions, so real users can sign in from day one. Workflows come as the next step, either by prompting the Co-Builder again or by adding them in the visual editor.

The Free plan is $0 for unlimited apps, with room for 5 app users, 3 builders, 5,000 records and 5 AI credits each month. Basic runs $19 per month, Pro $99 and Business $329, all on annual billing. Enterprise pricing is custom and includes SSO and SCIM for organizations managing access at scale. The full plan table sits on Softr's pricing page.

Lovable builds full-stack web apps through conversation, writing React code while it takes care of hosting, SSL and the backend. It's designed to move a founder or product team from a prompt to a shareable prototype, and the preview updates as the agent works.

Base44 calls itself a no-code AI platform: you explain the app, and it generates the structure, design and logic while running the backend itself. Authentication, a database and analytics come on every plan, Free included.

Base44 is free for 25 message credits a month. Starter costs $16 per month and Builder $40 per month, both on annual billing, with Pro at $80 and Elite at $160.

Bolt is a coding agent that writes full-stack projects in the browser and runs them on Bolt Cloud, which supplies hosting, databases and integrations. It suits people who want to watch the code take shape and edit it themselves.

Bolt Free is $0 with 1M tokens a month and Bolt branding. Pro costs $25 per month with 10M tokens, and Teams runs $30 a month for each member on monthly billing.

Replit pairs its Agent with a cloud workspace, so a description turns into a running app whose sign-in, database, hosting and monitoring come wired up. The homepage pitches it to people who don't code, though the result underneath is a code project.

Core costs $20 per month ($18 with annual billing) and Pro costs $100 per month ($90 with annual billing). Enterprise is custom and adds SSO, SCIM and audit logs.

Glide starts internal business apps from whatever you hand it, whether a spreadsheet, a written prompt or a PDF, and its newer platform, GlideOS, reads a sheet's structure and relationships to draft screens and workflows. Its apps grew up on phones, and that heritage still shapes the product.

Glide Free is $0 with limited credits. Basic costs $25 per month, Plus $50 per month and Pro $125 per month; annual billing isn't offered yet, according to Glide.

Bubble Free gives you 50K workload units and 500 AI credits each month. Web plans run $32 per month for Starter, $119 for Growth and $349 for Team, all on annual billing.

Retool is an internal tools engine aimed at engineering teams. You describe or assemble an app that connects straight to production databases, APIs and LLMs, with auth and access controls in place at deployment.

v0 generates full-stack Next.js web apps from a prompt and deploys them to Vercel, with Design Mode for visual tweaks and GitHub sync for the code. It's designed around a developer's workflow.

v0's Free plan gives new users $5 of one-time credits and caps messages at 7 per day. Plus runs $30 a month per user, and Business $100 a month per user.

Figma Make turns prompts and Figma design context into code-backed prototypes and web apps you can publish at a URL. It can also scaffold a Payload backend with a database, APIs, auth and an admin panel, or connect to Supabase for sign-in and stored data.

Figma's Starter plan is free with up to 500 AI credits a month. Figma lists a Professional Full seat at $16 per month with 3,000 AI credits, and Organization Full seats cost $55 per month on annual billing.

Collins Dictionary named "vibe coding" its Word of the Year for 2025, and the phrase now covers any workflow where you describe software in plain language and an AI produces it. AI app builders package that workflow with hosting, data and sign-in, so a non-developer can get from idea to something clickable without installing anything.

The questions buyers ask have turned plainer since then: what each edit costs, who keeps the app running, and whether a client can sign in and see their own records alone. A generated front end makes a good demo, and a team using it every day needs permissions and a predictable bill for the next hundred edits.

Softr is now my default when recommending AI app builders. It handles teams, auth, and real company data better than any other product. The AI Co-Builder drafts tables, pages and user groups, workflows follow as the next step, and the app keeps running on maintained platform components with no codebase to patch. App-user pricing and an editor that works without AI credits keep later changes from turning into a running meter.

Three situations call for a different tool. Glide makes a better mobile-first choice when the app spends its days on phones in the field. Bubble gives more front-end design freedom when a project calls for bespoke layouts, interactive animation or heavy brand styling. If your engineers want version control and audit logs and can handle a technical setup, Retool covers that need.

Agents are moving inside the apps these tools produce. Gartner expects task-specific AI agents inside 40% of enterprise applications by the close of 2026, against under 5% in 2025, so a 2027 app builder will need to host agents that respect the same permissions as human users.

Building is also leaving the builder's own window. The Model Context Protocol now sits under the Linux Foundation's Agentic AI Foundation and reports more than 97 million SDK downloads a month, with client support in ChatGPT and Claude, among others. Softr's changelog dates its official Claude and ChatGPT connector to October 6, 2026, and I expect more teams to start an app from the chat assistant they already use.

The caution comes from Gartner too: it predicts organizations will scrap upward of 40% of agentic AI projects before 2028, citing escalating costs, unclear business value and inadequate risk controls. Going into 2027, I'd judge any builder on whether it keeps agent costs and access rules visible to the team running the app.

Neville Naidoo is a technology journalist covering AI, startups, and the digital economy, with a focus on how emerging technologies are constantly evolving and shaping our everyday life.

#10702. Elon Musk once again promises Tesla’s new autonomous driving is better than humans

Elon Musk has a new promise: Version 15 of Full Self-Driving drives better than a real driver. “Completely new architecture that deserves being called ‘super’ intelligence, as it drives better than human intelligence,” he wrote on X after Tesla started sending the update to a first batch of owners.

That’s great news.

It was great news back in November 2023 too, when he wrote: “Over time, Version 12 (end-to-end neural nets) will far exceed human safety even when unsupervised.” Also in August 2025, when he said: “v14 will be better than human for sure, but I don’t know if it will be 10X. Maybe 2X to 3X. v15 has a shot at 10X.” Back then, he offered no data, and no third-party study to support his claim. It’s the same thing now.

Tesla says that the new FSD v15—which Musk says goes to wide release in December—upgrades the neural network that processes the camera sensors into information for the car to drive. It has also swapped the old lo-fi grayscale schematic 3D view for an AI-generated live street scene that looks like GTA VI-ish. The latter is the only thing that we know is real—and I have to admit it looks pretty neat—but nobody knows if the new driving engine works as well as Musk claims.

Same song, new version number

It may not matter too much. The new v15 is still Level 2 autonomous driving, which means drivers will need to watch the road at all times. While Musk says it drives better than a human, it must be a very dumb human because Tesla’s own support page says the software “does not make the vehicle autonomous” and is meant for use “only with a fully attentive driver.” That matters because, when a person sits ready to grab the wheel, every avoided crash could be the human’s save as much as the machine’s.

Tesla claims that the new FSD is “25x better handling of safety critical cases.” Better than what? Phil Duan, who leads self-driving at Tesla, said on X that “v15 is 25x safer than v14 across the fleet.” Musk answered an X user too: “Some people thought we meant 25% improvement, no we mean 25 times!” But we don’t really know about how much “safer” v14 was either. As electric mobility site Electrek points out, without real published figures or a peer-reviewed study—like this on Waymo reducing its crashes with injured people by 85%—that “25x” claim is hard to substantiate.

In a post, the company explains that its new vision encoder—the part of the network that interprets the information from the car’s video cameras—as the reason for that “25x better handling.” Now shared by FSD, Robotaxis, and Optimus humanoids, the engineering team says the new software improves “the critical intervention rate by 25x.” Tesla says the new encoder was trained on “millions of internet videos.”

According to Tesla, v15 adjusts its speed better in bad weather and school zones, steers clear of deep standing water, and is quicker to spot signs, potholes, small animals, chains and junk on the road. It doesn’t say anything about fake Looney Tunes’ painted tunnels.

It can ease off for speed cameras. And it learns from you, so if you cancel the slowdown at the same speed camera twice, by accelerating, it will stop braking at that point of the road. The company also claims that the car’s interior camera—which checks if you are paying attention to the road or not—now works more often when you wear hats and sunglasses.

Neat!

As part of the update, Tesla Reality View has changed. It now looks very similar to the live situational awareness screens you get in some Chinese cars: a hyperrealistic view of the road and your car as seen from behind at a 45-degree angle. You can see vehicles and people in 3D hyperrealistic renderings. Even the buildings are textured and lit like the real thing. It feels like a high-end car racing game. Musk claims “it’s like GTA.”

It looks neat, but it doesn’t seem to convey information better than the older user interface, which showed a much clearer representation of reality. Seen from a high angle, it was a simplified, very stylized version of the road populated with semi-transparent versions of vehicles and nearby people. In the old version, despite its perception problems, it was easy to get instant situational awareness. In the new v15, the realism obscures the information with too much darkness and glitz.

The interface also features a blue ribbon that traces the route FSD intends to drive, so you can correct it. Chevron arrows float overhead before every turn. And the map now shows several parking spots at your destination. Some Chinese car manufacturers are already displaying this information directly on the windshield, projecting computer-generated wide-screen AR graphics over the real world.

Fortunately, you can switch to a split view or just pick the GPS map view. Musk claims “you can tap any point on the reality revisualizer to make your car go there.” 

Musk says that the new UI uses the same visual features the FSD v15 driving model uses: It’s all AI generated. The AI model renders short bursts of eight frames at 36 frames per second, using just half of one of the accelerators in the car’s AI4 computer.

Still, the video may lag behind reality because the AI is not fast enough. Musk admits the lag is “a bit high due to compute constraints.” He claims Tesla has a way to trim it by about 35%. More promises.

If December’s wide release arrives on time (which may or may not happen), it will put the new FSD in a lot more hands. In 2027 we will be able to test the real value of Musk’s promises once again. Let’s stay positive. Maybe this will be it. You know what they say: 15th time’s the charm.

#10698. How Nvidia employees feel about Jensen Huang’s return

The public reaction to Jensen Huang’s return has been mixed at best, raising the hackles of lawmakers and corporate governance experts. Even the stock price took a bit of a dip after surging by over 30% on Monday. Within the company, however, the mood appears to be considerably lighter. 

In videos circulating across social media, Huang received a hero’s welcome at Nvidia headquarters, where his employees lined the walls and staircases as they cheered for him. Between chants of “Jensen! Jensen!” and “We love you Jensen,” he addressed his staff in a display of contrition. 

“I’m terribly sorry for putting all of you through all of this,” he said. “I’ve made some very terrible mistakes, and I’m going to work on coming back.” After an employee threw her arms around Huang in a tight hug—lasting over 10 seconds—he continued: “Thank you all for the second chance. I’m going to be a better CEO the second time around.”

Many Nvidia employees have taken to LinkedIn to express their relief over Huang’s return, seemingly overlooking the indiscretions that led to his ousting. They heaped praise on their newly reinstated CEO, describing him as the “heartbeat of Nvidia” and “one of a kind.” 

One employee even invoked Charles Dickens, paraphrasing the opening of A Tale of Two Cities: “It was the worst of times, it is the best of times. It was the season of Darkness, it is the season of Light. It was the winter of despair, it is the spring of hope. We had nothing before us, we have everything before us.”

Another employee claimed he always knew Huang would return to Nvidia: “When Jensen stepped down, many said their goodbyes and thanked him for his legacy. I stayed quiet—not out of indifference, but because deep down I knew there is only one boss, and he would be back.”

A former robotics engineer at Nvidia who quit rather publicly after Huang’s resignation congratulated him and admitted he made a hasty decision. “Looking back, I may have moved too quickly, and I don’t want to rush into another major decision. For now, I’d like to take some time to rest, reflect, and think carefully about my future. As part of that, I’m open to exploring the possibility of returning.”

These reactions line up with previous reports about employee sentiment after Huang stepped down: According to Wired, many employees showed immediate support for him internally, asking the board to reverse the decision. More than 10,000 current Nvidia employees, or about a quarter of its workforce, signed a petition to reinstate Huang as CEO.

Still, there are some employees who seem to feel differently—perhaps those who have been the target of Huang’s outbursts and creative insults, as reported by the New York Times recently. 

In a Wired report this week, several current and former employees expressed frustration over the turn of events, in part because they had hoped Huang’s exit would usher in some long overdue changes. A number of employees were disturbed by Huang’s affair with an alleged Chinese spy and at least one inappropriate relationship with an employee. 

Beyond that, however, they also felt that Nvidia condoned abusive behavior and echoed previous claims that Huang belittled employees at times. (In an internal post, one employee asserted: “If Jensen comes back, I’m not doing any HR trainings ever again.”) Workers also told Wired that the focus on unity and the “one team” ethos discouraged employees from challenging leadership.

Even employees who have few misgivings about the culture may now find themselves questioning Huang’s leadership. One engineering leader openly grappled with his feelings about Huang in a LinkedIn post, before he was reinstated. “Some believe his contributions to NVIDIA and the industry are so extraordinary that he deserves another chance,” he wrote. “Others argue that making exceptions for powerful leaders sets a dangerous precedent. I’ve wrestled with this question all week. My opinion has probably changed every single day. And I’ve realized something. I don’t have a clear answer.”

As evidenced by the reception to Huang yesterday, this may not be the prevailing view among employees. But whether or not other employees have lost faith in Huang, there are now open questions about how—if at all—the company plans to address the myriad issues that have been exposed by the events of the last few weeks.

#10695. Americans are sick of disruptive leaders, data shows

Business culture has long lionized bold, disruptive leaders. But these days, at least, Americans are yearning for some stability.   

In a survey conducted shortly after the midterm elections, 82% of Americans said they prefer leaders who “get things back to normal rather than shake things up” and 82% also said they prefer a “stable and predictable” life.

The global public relations firm Burson interviewed about 1,600 Americans across party lines. The poll was published November 14.

Ninety percent of respondents also said they want honest, calm, consistent leadership (90%), and 75% said they lose respect for CEOs who change positions under pressure.

It is easy to interpret these findings as a reflection of current public opinion about President Donald Trump. Trump’s penchant for disruption and brash businessman image helped him win the White House twice, but the outcome of the midterms—and the results of the Burson survey—indicate that the public has grown tired of the chaos.

A survey of roughly 100,000 midterm voters found that about two-thirds thought Trump had the wrong priorities, while seven in 10 said Congress hasn’t been doing enough to check the president’s power.

As Fast Company reported earlier this month, the midterm results also illustrate the risks organizations run when they become overly dependent on a charismatic leader. A leader’s success can make subordinates increasingly reluctant to question their judgment, even when circumstances change.

“At some point, you have to ask whether people are following your vision, or simply afraid to challenge it,” leadership expert Adam Mendler said.

#10689. Trump is deploying a special ‘Tiger Team’ for Thanksgiving air travel. Here’s why

Recent chaos is making Americans wary of braving air travel this holiday season, but the Trump administration says it has a plan.

The White House recently announced the formation of a “tiger team” dedicated to preventing major mess-ups like the recent multi-hour ground stop, which snarled airports nationwide as a mountain of delayed and rescheduled flights piled up. The mid-November incident lasted over four hours, grounding planes around the country as the federal government scrambled to get the standard lines of communications for air traffic controllers back online. 

Trump’s special interagency group known as the “Transportation Technology Transformation Tiger Team” will bring together representatives from the FAA, the Department of Transportation, the Department of Defense, and the Office of the Director of National Intelligence, according to the White House. “As millions of Americans prepare to travel for the Thanksgiving holiday, President Donald J. Trump is making sure the skies stay open and the systems run smoothly,” the White House said in a press release.

The FAA reported that 12,462 flights were canceled during the ground stop, which began mid-afternoon on Friday, November 13. The FAA is still investigating the technical hiccup that prompted thousands of planes to be grounded, but a routine router upgrade completed by federal contractor L3Harris Technologies in Arkansas is believed to be at fault.

Tiger teams in action

The term “tiger team” is sometimes used in corporate settings, but it is more commonly used to describe small, highly-skilled teams convened to solve difficult problems in government and military operations. The most famous tiger team in history is credited with safely guiding NASA’s Apollo 13 mission back to Earth safely, a feat that earned NASA Flight and Mission Operations Director Gene Kranz and his specialized group a Presidential Medal of Freedom. 

Other U.S. agencies also use tiger teams for specialized problem solving, including the U.S. Department of Labor. The Trump administration appears to have a particular affinity for the term and also stood up a self-described tiger team across the Department of Energy and the Department of the Interior last year to “unleash” the American energy industry. That group later became bogged down wrestling with oil prices and inflation tied to the war in Iran.

Beyond being tough to say five times fast, we don’t know a lot about Trump’s transportation tiger team. A White House official told The Daily Wire that the FAA is looking into “near-term” network configuration options and other short term ways to boost the stability of current air traffic control systems while slower modernization efforts move forward. 

“President Trump and Secretary Duffy have brought together the best and the brightest across the government to ensure what happened last Friday can never happen again,” White House spokeswoman Allison Schuster said. “…This team has hit the ground running in modernizing and improving the reliability of the nation’s decades-old air traffic control system.” 

Schuster claimed that American skies are already safer thanks to the “tremendous progress” from the tiger team, but reassuring Americans skittish about air safety after a spate of recent scares may not be that easy. Modernizing the U.S. air traffic control system is a major long-term goal shared by multiple presidential administrations and scores of lawmakers, but the ground stop showed that pushing technology fixes in an ancient system that never goes offline poses its own set of risks.

Record holiday travel expected

In a press release, the U.S. Department of Transportation said it expects to see the “busiest holiday skies in 16 years” with more than 417,000 flights forecast for the nine days beginning on Monday of Thanksgiving week. Tuesday is projected to be the busiest travel day of the period, with more than 52,000 flights scheduled. 

“From standing up a Tiger Team to ensure a seamless travel experience for fliers to using a successful new platform to predict and address congestion in the airspace, we’ve taken unprecedented action to limit flight delays and cancellations,” U.S. Transportation Secretary Sean P. Duffy said. “The American people can rest assured we will be on the clock 24/7 to get families to their destinations safely and on time.”

#10688. Top 6 AI Detector Tools in 2026: Features, Accuracy Claims and When to Trust Them

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI writing tools are now part of everyday work for students, marketers, developers and editors, and a parallel category of tools has appeared to answer one question: was this text written by a person or a model? Teachers, publishers, hiring teams and content managers use these detectors to screen submissions, but the technology is probabilistic, its accuracy claims vary, and the cost of a wrong call can be high.

This guide compares six AI detector tools, CudekAI, GPTZero, Copyleaks, Originality.ai, Turnitin and Sapling, on what their own pages state. It then explains how detectors work, summarizes the research on their limits, and ends with a short script you can use to test any detector on your own samples. The tools are listed in no ranking order. Feature details, limits and prices change often, so confirm current information on each vendor’s site before you commit.

• A detector gives a probability, not proof. Every vendor in this list warns that results can be wrong, and several advise against using a score alone.

• Limits differ widely. Character limits, supported languages, models detected and free allowances vary between tools and even between pages of the same tool.

• Bias and short text are known weak spots. Research has found detectors misclassify some non-native English writing, and most vendors say accuracy improves with longer text.

• Vendor accuracy claims are not comparable. Figures come from different datasets, thresholds and models, so test on your own material.

• Use detection as one signal. Combine it with drafts, version history, interviews or other evidence before acting on a result.

CudekAI’s AI detector returns an AI percentage and a human percentage for pasted text, with paragraph, sentence and word-level analysis for mixed documents. According to its page, it also accepts PDF and Word files with per-page scores and downloadable reports, and offers separate code and image detectors. The page lists models it says it detects, including ChatGPT, Gemini, Claude, Llama, DeepSeek and Grok, and says it supports more than 100 languages.

Strengths: sentence-level highlighting, file uploads with reports, and extra detectors for code and images in the same product.

Limitations and gaps: the page shows different character limits in different places, including 1,000, 5,000 and 15,000, so check the limit that applies to your plan. It says to start free but does not state a free allowance, and it lists detection as costing one credit. A headline accuracy figure of 99 percent appears in its page metadata, while the body refers to independent benchmarking without naming sources, and its FAQ says accuracy may vary for mixed or paraphrased text and that false positives can occur. API access is listed under an enterprise plan.

Fits: writers and teams who want a quick check with sentence-level detail and file reports, and who will verify results rather than rely on a single percentage.

GPTZero gives an overall score and highlights flagged sentences, and adds a Writing Replay feature that records the writing process, including typing patterns and paste events. It also lists a plagiarism checker and integrations with Google Docs, Google Classroom, Canvas, Zapier and a Chrome extension, plus an API.

Strengths: process-based evidence through Writing Replay, strong education integrations and published benchmark figures. On the RAID benchmark, the page reports 95.7 percent of AI text detected and 1 percent of human text wrongly flagged.

Limitations and gaps: it claims full support for five languages (English, German, Portuguese, French and Spanish) and says other languages lack stated accuracy levels. The company’s own page says no detector is 100 percent accurate, that results should not be the final verdict, and that accuracy is higher on longer English text. The free scan box has a 10,000-character limit, and plan prices are not listed on the page.

Fits: educators and editors who want writing-process evidence as well as a score.

Copyleaks describes an AI detector covering more than 30 languages and models including ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, DeepSeek and Llama. Its AI Logic feature explains why text was flagged, and it says it can detect AI-generated code. It offers a browser extension, a Google Docs add-on, learning-management-system integrations and an enterprise API.

Strengths: explanation of flagged text, code detection, broad integrations and published per-language accuracy figures, which range from 93.08 percent for Portuguese to 99.20 percent for English on AI text, according to the page.

Limitations and gaps: the figures are the vendor’s own. It claims a 0.03 percent false-positive rate and cites a 2023 study, so treat the number as a claim to verify on your data. Free scans cover up to 25,000 characters, but paid plan prices sit on a separate pricing page.

Fits: organizations that need multilingual coverage, code detection or integrations.

Originality.ai combines AI detection with a plagiarism checker and a fact and hallucination checker. It lists sentence-level highlights, a mixed AI and human category in its newer detection models, a Chrome extension with a writing replay feature and an AI detection API.

Strengths: several checks in one product, an API with documentation and a clear statement of limits. The page acknowledges false positives, says very short text under a 100-word minimum and AI rewriting tools can affect results, and advises against using the score alone.

Limitations and gaps: it is credit-based, free scans are capped at 100 credits per account and credits expire two years after purchase. Its accuracy claims cite third-party studies and internal benchmarks that you should review yourself.

Fits: publishers, agencies and SEO teams that want detection, plagiarism and fact checks in one workflow.

Turnitin is built for educators and institutions. Its AI writing detection estimates how much of a submission is human-written versus likely AI-generated, including likely AI-paraphrased content, and works inside learning-management systems alongside similarity checking.

Strengths: deep institutional integration and unusually explicit framing of results as probabilities. For documents above its 300-word minimum, the page reports false-positive rates of 0.014 for English language learners and 0.013 for native English writers and concludes there is no statistically significant bias. It also links guidance on handling suspected false positives.

Limitations and gaps: it is aimed at institutions rather than individual freelancers, the page does not give an overall false-positive figure, and it describes detection as just one piece of a broader challenge. Mention of language support is limited, with a linked post about Arabic.

Fits: schools and universities that already use Turnitin and want detection within the same workflow.

Sapling offers an AI content detector with a free check of the first 2,000 characters, and Pro and Enterprise accounts can check up to 100,000 characters. It provides a free API key and documentation, and it publishes unusually candid guidance on limits.

Strengths: transparent caveats and a developer-friendly API. The page says false positives and negatives may occur, that accuracy varies with dataset, text length and threshold, and advises measuring results on your own examples. It also warns that some tools claiming to be undetectable falsely say they passed its detector.

Limitations and gaps: accuracy improves after about 50 words, non-English text is scored by the English model for unsupported languages and is described as less reliable, and prices are not listed on the page. A reported 60 percent drop in false positives comes from internal evaluations that the page does not publish.

Fits: developers and teams that want to integrate detection and test it on their own data.

The table summarizes what each page states. It is not a benchmark, and “not stated” means the page did not say, not that the feature is absent.

• False positives. Human writing can be flagged, especially when it is short, formulaic or highly predictable.

• Bias risk. A research paper titled GPT detectors are biased against non-native English writers found that the detectors it tested consistently misclassified non-native English writing as AI-generated, and warned against using such detectors in educational settings. Turnitin’s page reports the opposite for its own tool, which is a reason to test on your population.

• Evasion. Paraphrasing or editing AI text can lower scores. The same research found that simple prompting strategies could both reduce bias and get text past detectors.

• Mixed authorship. A document that is partly AI-assisted can get a low overall score while individual sentences score high, as Sapling’s page points out.

• Moving targets. New models change the text distribution, so a tool trained on older models can lose accuracy.

• Shifting benchmarks. Vendors report different datasets and thresholds, so numbers are not comparable.

OpenAI’s own experience is a useful reminder. Its AI text classifier correctly flagged 26 percent of AI-written text on a challenge set while mislabeling 9 percent of human text, and the page states that the classifier was withdrawn on July 20, 2023 due to its low rate of accuracy. Detection has improved since, but the underlying difficulty has not gone away.

Most detectors are classifiers trained on large collections of human-written and machine-generated text. They look for statistical patterns, such as how predictable the word choices are or how uniform the sentence structure is, and output a probability. Some systems add features such as sentence-level scoring, writing-process data, or checks for hidden provenance signals.

• Statistical signals. Text that a language model finds highly predictable tends to look machine-like, which is why formulaic human writing can be misflagged.

• Trained classifiers. Detectors learn from labeled examples and update as new models appear.

• Writing-process evidence. Tools such as Writing Replay record how a document was typed or pasted, which is stronger evidence than a style score.

• Provenance. Standards such as C2PA Content Credentials aim to record the origin and edits of content when the creating tool supports them, which is different from guessing after the fact.

Because vendor figures are not comparable, the most reliable approach is to measure. Build a small labeled set that reflects your real content, including human writing from different groups of authors, and calculate false-positive and false-negative rates by group. The script below wraps any detector behind a single function. The stub is a placeholder, so replace it with a call to the tool you are evaluating and follow that vendor’s API documentation.

is_ai: bool        # ground truth you control

group: str = "all" # e.g. "native", "non-native", "technical"

def evaluate(detect, samples, threshold=0.5):

"""detect(text) -> probability the text is AI-generated (0..1).

Wrap any vendor API or tool export in this function."""

g = stats.setdefault(s.group, {"fp": 0, "human": 0, "fn": 0, "ai": 0})

g["fn"] += not flagged          # AI text that slipped through

g["fp"] += flagged              # human text wrongly flagged

fpr = g["fp"] / g["human"] if g["human"] else float("nan")

fnr = g["fn"] / g["ai"] if g["ai"] else float("nan")

print(f"{group:12} false positive rate {fpr:.1%}  false negative rate {fnr:.1%}")

return 0.9 if "delve" in text.lower() else 0.2

Sample("Let us delve into the topic.", True, "native"),

Sample("I wrote this note by hand.", False, "native"),

Sample("We delve into methods used here.", False, "non-native"),

Sample("The results were reviewed twice.", True, "non-native"),

With only a handful of samples the numbers mean little, so use a larger, representative set and report results per group, because an acceptable overall rate can hide a high rate for one group.

• Never treat a score as proof. Use it to start a conversation, not end one.

• Gather corroborating evidence. Drafts, version history, writing-process data and discussion with the author carry more weight than a percentage.

• Tell people how detection is used. Publish a policy so authors know what to expect and how to appeal.

• Check for bias. Test on writers like those you evaluate, including non-native speakers.

A tool that estimates how likely a piece of text is to have been generated by an AI model, usually as a percentage, often with sentence-level highlighting.

They can be useful, but accuracy depends on the text, length, language and threshold, and every vendor in this guide states that errors can occur. Test on your own samples.

Yes. False positives are acknowledged by vendors and documented in research, particularly for short or formulaic text and some non-native English writing.

Free tiers are fine for a quick check, but they have limits on length and features, and the result should not be the sole basis for a decision.

Sometimes. Paraphrasing and editing can lower scores, and partly AI-generated documents can produce mixed results.

A plagiarism checker looks for matches with existing sources. An AI detector estimates whether text resembles machine-generated writing, which is a different and less certain task.

AI detectors are useful screening tools, but none is a verdict. The six reviewed here differ in language coverage, limits, integrations, explainability and how candidly their pages describe error, and their accuracy figures come from different tests. Compare them on the factors that matter for your content, measure false positives on your own samples, and combine any score with other evidence before acting on it.

#10686. Nintendo Switch 2-ready smart glasses from Viture are at their lowest price ever (up to $160 off)

Viture’s smart display glasses offer official Switch 2 compatibility with the Mobile Dock Mini, and these Black Friday deals even come with free bonus controllers and other accessories.

One of the most annoying things about playing Ocarina of Time right now is that everyone in my home is at a different point in their save file. This makes it tough to be in the same room with others and not spoil something for them, but plugging in a pair of Viture glasses makes this much easier since no one else can see the screen inside the glasses I’m wearing.

You can get your own pair of Viture glasses with the Switch 2 Mobile Dock Mini for as low as $375. This pack comes with Viture Pro 2 glasses, which sport a pair of 120Hz micro-OLED displays with 1,600-nit peak brightness and built-in myopia adjustment diopters. They also weigh just 63g, making them comfortable for long gaming sessions.

The Mobile Dock Mini (on sale for $99 on its own) in the pack comes with a magnetic mount that attaches to the back of the Switch 2. This Dock lets you plug your Viture glasses in and also charges the Switch 2, thanks to the built-in 10,000mAh battery. That delivers more than double the Switch 2’s battery life, so you can clear several temples in Ocarina before even thinking of stopping to charge.

Most of Viture’s glasses are on sale and have extra free gifts, which you can see in the image above. This includes the company’s high-end Viture Beast model, which is on sale for $489 ($60 off). That’s the lowest price ever for the glasses.

Viture Beast upgrades the experience with a wider field of view (58 degrees), native 3DoF spatial tracking, and 9-level electrochromic lens dimming. Native spatial tracking means that you can pin the virtual monitor in space and move around as if it were a TV mounted on a wall. Beast doesn’t have diopters, so you’ll need to order prescription lenses if you need myopia adjustment.

The biggest discount of all is on Viture Luma Pro, which is down to $339 ($160 off). Luma Pro is Viture’s “Goldilocks” pair with excellent image quality and RGB lighting along the frames for some extra style, and even includes myopia adjustment diopters. Pair any of these Viture glasses with the Mobile Dock Mini for Switch 2 compatibility, making them the only pair of smart glasses with official Switch 2 support.

Lastly is Viture Luma Ultra, down to $449 ($150 off). Luma Ultra sports the best image quality of all Viture glasses and includes myopia-adjusting diopters. These are the only pair that support 6DoF tracking when using the Viture Spacewalker app thanks to the cameras on the front, but there’s no native 3DoF support like Beast has, so this functionality is limited to content you view through the app.

#10682. Samsung might copy the iPhone’s Dynamic Island soon, but it actually looks good [Video]

A new leak suggests that Samsung is preparing to copy the iPhone’s Dynamic Island feature in the not-too-distant future, to an extent that not many other Android brands have even done thus far.

Copying the Dynamic Island was briefly like a rite of passage for some Android brands, with many wasting almost no time in delivering their own version of the feature. But, on many of those devices, it’s a surface-level ripoff at best. The feature is primarily limited to music apps and timers, rarely working with any third-party apps in meaningful ways. Android has a built-in feature similar to Apple’s but not in the same styling.

Samsung is now preparing its own version of the Dynamic Island, and it seems this one has a bit more meat on its bones.

SamMobile on Twitter/X posted a video and two still images of what appears to be a Dynamic Island clone coming to Galaxy devices. It’s mentioned as potentially coming to the Galaxy S27 series (so, One UI 9.5) and being called “Moment Effects.” A portion of the software explicitly mentions integration with Google Sports, the leak claims, while it seems Samsung is using Live Notifications here, which already support a number of third-party apps.

The current implementation of live activities showing the "chip" in the top bar is honestly far from good.

It negates one of the major pros of Android over iOS: being able to see notification icons in the top bar.

As when the "chip" of the live activity is active/shown, all notification icons will get hidden, even if there's a ton of space left between the "chip" and the constraint of the front camera area - an implementation that makes no sense whatsoever.

I honestly can't fathom how could Google come out with such poor implementation.

I don't particularly like this one from Samsung, but it surely is 1000000 times better from a UX perspective.

The kicker from a UX perspective is that Samsung’s interpretation allows you to swipe between activities, similar to Apple, but the core premise is similar with the island showing around the selfie camera cutout.

When this feature might surface does remain unclear, but a One UI 9.5 debut would make plenty of sense. That update is in beta now and due to launch on the Galaxy S27 series early next year.

What do you think of Samsung’s alleged Dynamic Island clone? Personally, I’m on board as long as it proves useful, and it seems to be on the right track compared to most other copycats.

Apple's base MacBook Pro in the citrus colorway, with an A18 Pro chip, 8GB of RAM, 256GB of storage, and no Touch ID option.
