#10679. Galaxy Z Fold 8 features: What you can do on a foldable

Folding phones have come in numerous variations over the years as the concept has evolved. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 is the latest evolution that introduces a form factor that fits every user. Its unique design makes it a foldable most people can actually use – which makes it a perfect gift. You might be surprised by what it can do.

It’s in the name. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 folds and unfolds like other foldable devices – that much you know. But what the Galaxy Z Fold 8’s form factor provides is something different to what most might be familiar with.

Instead of a tall device that unfolds into a near-uniform square display, you get a wider screen more like a tablet. That makes it perfect for watching videos or movies. Content that naturally works well on TVs or your computer will look great on the Galaxy Z Fold 8.

What about content that’s best viewed in portrait mode? Because it opens up to a tablet-like display, you can turn the Fold 8 to watch YouTube Shorts or Instagram Reels without a problem, so if you or someone you know likes to watch content on a bigger display, the Z Fold 8 makes that much easier. It really doesn’t matter what content you come across – the wider inner display gives you that large screen feeling.

Like a well-designed tool, the Galaxy Z Fold 8 has more purposes than the tasks best suited for the inner display. That shorter, wider profile makes the outer display smaller, but not as thin as something on other foldables, like the Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra.

In reality, the aspect ratio is similar enough across both the inner and outer screens that most content scales well on the shorter front panel. Social apps like X and Instagram look great because videos and images are able to fill the screen, rather than squeeze. Samsung’s One UI software also supports app optimization across both screens, although the experience may vary by app.

Saying the Galaxy Z Fold 8’s outer display is “minimal” isn’t a dig, either. Apps can be refreshing to use when you don’t need to open them on a large scale. Take Messages, for instance. It’s not a complex app, and there isn’t a ton to see – just the last couple of messages exchanged. The same goes for plenty of apps available on the Google Play Store.

And guess what? If you don’t like using the app on a smaller display, just unfold the phone. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 is such a versatile phone, whether it’s a gift during the holidays or one for yourself.

There are instances where you need the large real estate. One UI 9 gives the Galaxy Z Fold 8 the option to open and run multiple apps on your screen. There are several ways you can do it, too.

The main approach is to split two apps on the wide inner display. But if you want to see more from one app, you can simply drag the split over in one direction. One UI 9 leaves room to add one more app to that split screen, giving you a three-way Split View. All it takes is dragging an app from the taskbar and choosing the spot.

If you truly need a fourth app open, you have the option to open another in a floating window. Having four apps open at once is definitely a niche instance, but the Galaxy Z Fold 8 handles it like it’s nothing. You can have YouTube up in one corner while you check Messages for gift ideas and shop through the Amazon app. It might be helpful to keep a list of what you got and for whom in a floating Sheets window.

The point is not only that it’s technically possible, but it’s usable. So often devices let users open apps in split screen, but are in no way comfortable enough for the task. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 is perfect for it, and the experience actually makes you more productive, not less.

Most phones don’t let you get the best angle without any sort of third-party accessory. The Galaxy Z Fold 8, though, acts like its own tripod in a way.

Let’s say you’re taking photos, and you need to get a shot of yourself, but no one is around to take the photo for you. The Galaxy Z Fold 8’s hinge lets it prop itself up, so you can do things like set a timer and run to strike a pose.

This comes in handy for videos, too. Think of how many times you wanted to get a good angle on a plane or somewhere where there’s this flat surface just asking for a tiny tripod or kickstand. Well, the Galaxy Z Fold 8 is its own kickstand and can be positioned at just the right angle.

You can get the Galaxy Z Fold 8 from your favorite carriers and retailers, but Best Buy is one of the leading places to find deals during the holiday season. You’ll find hefty discounts, higher trade-in values for your old devices, and more.

A tri-band Wi-Fi 7 router from a well-known brand at less than $100 is an absolute steal.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Over the past few years, as Wi-Fi 7 has proliferated through the consumer router market, we’ve routinely seen dual-band Wi-Fi 7 routers dip below $100, and in some cases, even below $80. While that pricing is great for those who want the lowest barrier to entry for Wi-Fi 7, those dual-band offerings lack the higher-performance 6 GHz band. That isn’t the case with the TP-Link Archer BE9500, which is currently available for an incredibly low $89.99 at Best Buy.

The TP-Link Archer BE9500 is a budget-friendly tri-band Wi-Fi 7 router delivering speeds up to 9.5 Gbps. It features an ultra-fast 10 Gbps WAN/LAN port, two 2.5 Gbps LAN ports, two 1 GbE LAN ports, and 320 MHz channel support on the 6 GHz band. It covers up to 2,600 sq. ft.

That price gets you a tri-band router, offering 1,032 Mbps speeds on the 2.4 GHz band, up to 2,722 Mbps on the 5 GHz band, and up to 5,776 Mbps on the 6 GHz band. That is a compelling wireless setup for less than $90, but it doesn't stop there. You'll find two 2.5 GbE LAN ports, two 1 GbE LAN ports, and even a 10 GbE WAN/LAN port for connecting to a high-speed fiber internet gateway. The fact that we're also seeing 10 GbE at this price point is shocking, which makes this deal even more of an outlier in the Wi-Fi 7 router market.

I reviewed the very similar Archer BE9700 back in 2025. While they look identical from the outside, the Archer 9700 includes four 2.5 GbE LAN ports instead of just two. When we tested the Archer 9700, we saw just over 2,500 Mbps speeds in iPerf3 at close range. The Archer BE9500 should offer similar wireless speeds.

Other items of note with the Archer BE9500 are that it includes a USB 3.0 port for hooking up a printer or storage device, it features six poseable antennas, and can be easily configured using the TP-Link WebGUI on a desktop or the Tether app on your smartphone.

This $89.99 price is advertised as an early Black Friday special, so we suggest that you strike now before Best Buy runs out of stock, or the price shoots back up to its usual $100+ realm.

#10675. Gemini app rolls out very slick camera redesign on Android

Google has thoroughly redesigned Gemini for Android’s in-app camera that lets you quickly add an image to prompts.

Available from the ‘plus’ menu in the prompt box, the old interface launched a fullscreen viewfinder that was quite generic. This UI is still in use when you launch the camera from the Gemini overlay.

The new one appears inline on the Gemini homepage or inside a conversation. It launches as a bottom sheet that you can drag up — with a really slick animation — to take up most of the screen.

This nearly edge-to-edge redesign, which extends behind the system navigation bar, is a stark contrast to the old approach.

To the right of the capture button is a three-dot overflow button that animates on open, revealing options to control the flash and switch to the front-facing camera. There’s a back button on the other side, but you can also just swipe down.

This camera redesign first launched in the Gemini for iOS app, but has now widely rolled out on Android in both the beta and stable (version 17.76+) channels. Be sure to Force stop the Google app if you’re not seeing it live yet.

This follows the launch of GIF creation in the Gemini app yesterday. Powered by Gemini Omni 1.2 Flash, select GIF from the Format menu or specify “Create a GIF” in your prompt. Google frames this feature as an alternative to searching for GIFs.

Say hello to GIFs in Gemini. Say goodbye to searching for the perfect one.From looping photo effects to funny memes, create any GIF you can imagine with our latest video model, Gemini Omni 1.2 Flash, for smooth motion and consistent quality across every frame.

#10672. The Google Home app is better than Nest alone ever was, mostly

The Nest app is officially dead, but it’s worth taking a step back and remembering that, while there are some rough edges, the Google Home app is a better smart home experience than Nest alone ever could have been.

9to5Google Weekender by Ben Schoon is a part of our free newsletter that also includes ‘Inbox,’ a twice-weekly roundup of key stories from around the web by Daniel Bader. Sign up here to get Weekender delivered to your inbox every Sunday along with ‘Inbox’ on Tuesday and Thursday. You can find past issues here.

The Nest app has been on life support for years now. Google has been migrating legacy products into the Home app for a while and the last few puzzle pieces came in Nest Protect support, the death of the earliest Nest Thermostats, and other assorted adjustments.

Realistically, even long-time Nest users have had no reason to continue using the app over Google Home.

Having been personally, deeply invested in Google’s vision for the smart home over the past decade, I’ll say that the Google Home app we have today is better than the Nest app ever was or ever could be. A huge part of that comes from Google Home’s ability to pull together devices from third-party ecosystems. Being able to control your entire smart home from a single app is how the smart home always should have been, and between Google’s work and the Matter standard, we’re finally able to enjoy that pretty seamlessly for the most part. Automations, Google’s actually-useful AI features, and more only sweeten the deal. I’m also a big fan of what the Home app has become from a UX perspective. While there are always rough edges and bugs to clean up, the customizable homepage and well-organized sections make for a good – not perfect, but good – experience for the end user.

That said, there is one thing that the Nest app’s limited scope benefitted from, and that’s a focus on making your own product great.

Google’s move to the Home app for Nest devices has been a 5-year run of trying to clean things up, improve on the app, and basically bring back the way things were in the Nest app as far as the first-party cameras and thermostats are concerned. It’s taken ages, but we’re finally up to speed, mostly. I still hate that you have to reset Nest Cams to change Wi-Fi when the Nest app just let you change it, and the Nest app’s settings were much less overwhelming, but I’d say the pros far outweigh the cons at this point.

The Nest app was good at its job, but the Google Home app is a worthy successor, finally, and now the Nest app can finally rest. I mostly think the same applies to Gemini vs Google Assistant, with the newer assistant a better overall experience, but it’s easier to poke holes in that argument.

Disagree? What do you think of the Google Home app, and were you still using the Nest app?

#10670. How Much Latency Does a Self-Hosted LLM Gateway Add? Langfuse vs LiteLLM vs AcruxCore

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A self-hosted LLM gateway sits between your app and the model provider, so every call pays for one extra hop. We measured that cost for three open-source gateways: the Langfuse AI Gateway, the LiteLLM proxy and AcruxCore. Each one ran on the same machine, in front of the same OpenAI model. LiteLLM added 61 to 67 ms to every non-streaming call. AcruxCore added 15 to 24 ms. Langfuse's number could not be told apart from zero.

The bigger lesson was about the measurement itself. Before we controlled for it, one OpenAI network address moved our results by more than any gateway did.

Each number is the median time a gateway added to a direct OpenAI call, with its 95% interval in brackets. A gateway can log each call, and logging takes time, so we ran the test three times with three logging levels.

The direct call took 781 to 831 ms at the median, so even LiteLLM's cost is under 9% of a short call. Read the tables this way:

The obvious test is to time 100 calls through the gateway, time 100 direct calls, and compare the two medians. That test gives the wrong answer, for two reasons.

The first reason is noise. OpenAI's own response time moved by hundreds of milliseconds between calls in our runs. The direct call's p95 was 1,047 to 1,233 ms against a median near 800 ms. A gateway that adds 20 ms is hidden inside that spread unless the test removes it.

The second reason is the network path to OpenAI. It does not stay the same between a direct client and a gateway, and that difference can be larger than the gateway itself.

api.openai.com resolves to more than one address. From our network, DNS returned two Cloudflare addresses, and one was much farther away than the other. We measured both with a script that pins one address at a time:

On a connection that is already open, both addresses answer in the same time. On a new connection, the far address adds about 250 ms, because the TCP and TLS handshakes take that much longer.

A gateway keeps its connection to OpenAI open between calls. It pays the setup once and then stays on whichever address it reached first. A client that opens a new connection for each call pays the setup again on every call. How much it pays depends on which address DNS returns that time.

Node's built-in fetch closes an idle connection after about 4 seconds. So a test client that calls OpenAI every few seconds reconnects on almost every call. In our first, uncontrolled run, the Langfuse gateway came out 43 ms faster than calling OpenAI directly. A gateway cannot be faster than the call it forwards. The direct client paid for new connections, and the gateway did not.

The fix has two parts. First, pin api.openai.com to one address on every path. In Docker, that is an extra_hosts entry on each gateway container:

Second, let the direct client keep its connection open, as a gateway does. With undici, the HTTP client behind Node's fetch, that is a custom Agent:

We then listed each container's open connections to confirm that every path used the same address. The edge-check script is in the repository linked at the end, and it takes about a minute to run from your own network.

This advice also applies outside benchmarks. If your app calls OpenAI directly from Node, keep the connection open between calls. Otherwise some of your calls pay the handshake, and on a far address that costs about 250 ms.

Each test sent 150 rounds after 5 warm-up rounds. One round calls OpenAI directly and through each gateway, once without streaming and once with streaming. The order rotates every round, so no path always goes first or last.

For each round, the script takes the gateway's time minus the direct call's time in that same round. Calls in one round are made within a few seconds of each other, so a slow moment at OpenAI hits all four paths together and cancels out of the difference. The script then reports the median of the 150 differences.

The interval comes from a bootstrap. The script draws 150 differences at random, with replacement, and takes their median. It repeats that 2,000 times, and the middle 95% of those medians is the interval. This is a shortened version of the script's function:

An interval that includes zero means the test could not separate the gateway's cost from zero. That is what the Langfuse rows show. It does not prove that the cost is zero.

Every call used OpenAI gpt-6-luna with reasoning_effort: "none", the same short prompt and a cap of 5 output tokens. A short answer keeps the model's own time small, so more of the measured time belongs to the path. Each streamed call measured the time to its first token. Each non-streamed call measured the time to the full response.

All three gateways did real work on every call, which made the comparison fair:

The three logging levels mapped to these settings:

The machine was an AMD Ryzen 9 5900X with 62 GB of RAM, running Fedora Linux and Docker 29.9. All three stacks ran on it at once, and the test was run on 10 November 2026.

LiteLLM added 61 to 67 ms to the full response, but only 9 to 43 ms to the first streamed token. The first token arrives as soon as the model starts to answer, so time spent before that point shows up in both numbers. The larger cost without streaming suggests that most of LiteLLM's extra time is spent after the model has answered. We did not profile LiteLLM to confirm this.

Logging level made no clear difference for any gateway. Adding LiteLLM's Langfuse callback did not raise its cost. AcruxCore's median rose from 15 ms with logging off to 24 ms with full logging, but its intervals overlap across all three runs. AcruxCore's median is 16 to 24 ms above Langfuse's, and those intervals overlap too, so this test does not rank the two of them.

If 60 ms per call matters to you, as in a chat app that makes several model calls per user message, LiteLLM's cost is real and repeatable. Langfuse and AcruxCore stayed near or within the noise. If your calls take several seconds, any of the three costs less than OpenAI's own variation from one call to the next.

Before you trust a gateway number, your own or anyone else's, check two things. Was each gateway compared with a direct call in the same round? And did the direct client keep its connection open on the same OpenAI address as the gateway? Without both, the result measures the network path and not the gateway.

The scripts, Compose files and raw results are on GitHub: scripts/comparison/langfuse-gateway-vs-litellm-vs-acruxcore.

Talha Anwar is a senior AI and full-stack engineer and writes about building and running AI systems.

#10667. Samsung Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 vs Watch 9: When is it right to spend more?

The Samsung Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 is worth spending more on if you want longer battery life, a brighter outdoor display, and specialized features for trail running and diving. The Galaxy Watch 9 is the better fit if you want Samsung’s latest everyday health tools in a smaller, lighter watch.

Both, however, are fantastic smartwatches with limitless potential. They share a Snapdragon Wear Elite processor and many of Samsung’s newest wellness features, so deciding between them is really about figuring out your priorities when it comes to buying a wearable this holiday season. We’re here to help you figure that out.

As we covered in our Galaxy Watch 9 and Watch Ultra 2 comparison, both watches move to Qualcomm’s Snapdragon Wear Elite platform. Both also ship with Wear OS 7 and One UI 8 Watch.

That shared foundation includes useful health insights. Vitals brings overnight measurements together to establish your personal baseline, while Daily Cardio Load helps you understand training demands and recovery. Hearing monitoring can flag potentially unsafe noise levels, whether you’re at a concert or somewhere unexpectedly loud.

Both also get five years of Wear OS updates and security patches, making either a useful long-term partner for your Samsung device, like the Galaxy S26 Ultra or Galaxy Z Fold8.

The clearest differences are size, battery capacity, display brightness, and outdoor capabilities. Here’s how the lineup breaks down:

As you can tell, the Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 is considerably bigger and heavier, but you get a lot more capability for that additional size.

The Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 makes its strongest case when your plans regularly take you beyond a desk, a gym, or a charger.

Samsung rates the Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 for up to 60 hours with the always-on display enabled, compared with up to 30 hours for the Watch 9, and that extra headroom is appealing when your day starts with a workout and finishes well into the evening.

Then there’s the screen. In our hands-on with both watches, the Ultra 2’s brightness stood out immediately, with a higher peak that gives it an advantage when checking workout information outdoors, even in the bright sun. And its titanium frame provides added durability.

The Ultra 2 also adds Trail Run tracking for elevation and terrain impact, route guidance using compatible GPX files, and Nutrition Alert hydration guidance. These are tangible reasons to upgrade if your usual run involves trails instead of pavement.

Diving is another differentiator. Samsung specifies recreational diving support to depths of up to 40 meters, with automatic tracking of depth, dive time and water temperature — a remarkable feat for a piece of technology that lives on your wrist.

The best choice becomes clearer when you picture your own routine:

Choose the Galaxy Watch 9 for everyday wellness, a lighter fit, and strong value. Choose the Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 for longer adventures, more demanding outdoor activities, and greater freedom between charges.

If you’re replacing an older Galaxy Watch, both offer a compelling new generation of health insights and long-term support. Both last a long time and look absolutely awesome with running shorts or a business suit. But the Watch Ultra 2 can take you that extra mile, literally and figuratively, and may be worth spending more on during Best Buy’s Holiday Deals event.

GTA V’s original PC release asks for a 1GB card; this one has a sixteenth of that.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

YouTube channel Budget-Builds Official has done the unthinkable, getting Grand Theft Auto V to run on a Radeon graphics card from 2007 with just 64MB of VRAM. The game is almost downright playable, too, with config edits, a move to Windows 8, and an overclock getting its speed up from just 1 FPS into the 13 to 20 FPS range, sans most cars, that is.

The channel detailed the feat, which targets a GPU with just a sixteenth of the video memory that’s usually required to play the game. GTA V Legacy lists, as its minimum, an Nvidia 9800 GT or AMD HD 4870, both cards with DirectX 10 support and 1GB of VRAM. The Enhanced version, released in 2025, needs 4GB, from a GTX 1630 or RX 6400 as specified.

In fact, the chosen card, an ATI Radeon HD 2350, is potentially the worst card that can launch GTA V, at least by the tester’s claim, even though its DDR2 memory is probably one of the few types not in short supply at the moment. Notably, this approach doesn’t involve using a unified or shared system memory AMD APU workaround; this is dedicated VRAM. It’s also not the first attempt at something like this, as the channel has an earlier run with 8MB of VRAM on games like Half-Life 2 and Minecraft.

Reaching the final settings took “the best part of half a day” of tweaking, after which it was possible to complete the prologue and the first mission. In some respects, the end result “genuinely does look like a PS2 game,” which probably wouldn’t actually faze fans of the series. However, for this specific title, the modder believes that “this is as low as you can actually go with GTA V.” Going any lower would likely mean reimagining the game in some way.

The game’s first launch with the card, on Windows 10 and the Catalyst 13.1 driver, led to a start screen hang of about 10 minutes. The game was expecting the previous HD 6900-series card, and a settings deletion plus relaunch got things back into action. One of the video’s chapter titles, “We are in GAME... at 1FPS,” summarizes the experience pretty well.

The plan was to use the lowest in-menu settings, of course, and then the config file from the channel’s earlier 256MB run with various settings: no shadows, no reflections, and a resolution around 240p. This got things to 3 FPS, a significant improvement, but far from good enough. Changes to level-of-detail values to their lowest levels got things up to 10 FPS. However, it wasn’t yet time to give up.

Downgrading to Windows 8 and Catalyst 12.6, “the original display drivers for Windows 8,” as well as an older build of the game, paved the pathway forward. Importantly, the OS exposed overclocking that wasn’t available in Windows 10, so a second fan went onto the card first, and the GPU’s core was pushable from 400MHz to 520MHz with the assistance of MSI Afterburner. Pushing the memory from 400MHz to 475MHz led to a black screen, and the video doesn’t say what the memory ended up at, though the card survived.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The Windows 8 setup reached 14 FPS, and further engine-file edits, to visual settings.dat and to level-of-detail options through OpenIV, put gameplay in the 13 to 20 FPS range.

Ignore the dots on the screen during gameplay; the tester says these are from being out of memory, verified by an artifact-free Unigine Valley run.

The game ended up playable, although, aside from the dots, it had many other visual issues: gaps between vertices in the world, and side characters had far fewer textures than the main ones. Additionally, most cars didn’t render, since keeping them loaded crashed the game with out-of-memory errors. This makes police “hover” into scenes and, hilariously, pop out of invisible cars. Likewise, the train in the prologue never actually loads. First-person driving, at least, works. All of this plays within that range, although busier city streets can drop from 15 down to 11 or 12 FPS.

The creator says the full gameplay will likely go up as its own video on a second channel, in “lossless quality.” The video is timely, acting as a send-off just before GTA VI’s release on Nov. 19, although not yet on PC, with a PC version reportedly due next year.

#10660. Which Samsung folding phone is right for you? Galaxy Z Fold 8, Ultra, or Flip 8

Folding phones are entering a new chapter and, if you’re looking to try one for the first time, you might not know exactly where to start. In this guide, we’ll take a look at some of the most important questions to answer when buying a foldable, and dissect how Samsung’s Galaxy Z Fold 8, Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra, and Galaxy Z Flip 8 fit the bill to figure out which is the best for you, especially if you’re interested in one this holiday season.

Samsung has three foldables in its lineup this year, two of which are pretty familiar. The Galaxy Z Flip 8 continues the lineup of clamshell-style foldables, offering a form factor similar to that of a classic flip phone. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra, meanwhile, is designed to be a normal smartphone on the outside, hiding a tablet within. Finally, the Galaxy Z Fold 8 is the new kid on the block, offering a compact outer display that opens up to a media-friendly, wider inner panel.

Each one serves a different need, but they all share similar performance and core specs. Here’s a quick rundown of the key details on each device:

Starting with the displays, the book-style Galaxy Z Fold 8 and Fold 8 Ultra both provide outer displays that are fully usable as standard smartphones, while the inner display is useful for multitasking, media, or just getting more space for reading a website or e-book. Samsung’s top-tier specs deliver performance for everything you’d need, with Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 5 for Galaxy powering everything from demanding games or media-editing apps to day-to-day use.

With each Samsung foldable, you’re getting a fully capable smartphone, but finding the one that’s best for you is a matter of picking your most important priorities. Cameras, battery life, content consumption, and size are some of the most important points to consider, so let’s take a look one-by-one.

When it comes to cameras, foldables have a tough time. There’s only so much physical space to fit the camera sensors and, with cameras, having more space generally results in a battery overall camera.

That said, the best camera on a Samsung foldable is undoubtedly on the Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra.

The Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra has a 200MP main camera that’s similar to the Galaxy S26 Ultra, while it also packs a 50MP ultrawide and something neither of the others offer, a telephoto lens. The 10MP telephoto shooter captures faraway subjects at 3x optical zoom, meaning you’re not giving up on quality when you zoom in. Two 10MP cameras can be found on the displays, with neither sacrificing quality for an under-display look.

Runner-up here is the Galaxy Z Fold 8, which offers a pair of 50MP cameras for wide and ultrawide shots. You can get 2x optical-quality zoom out of the main sensor to still get a good zoom shot, while the main camera still offers good quality and Samsung’s best camera features including 2x optical-quality zoom and the subject-tracking My FanCam.

The Galaxy Z Flip 8 may not have the best camera hardware, but what it lacks in hardware it makes up for in flexibility. This is definitely the best form factor of the bunch for using the foldable design as a tripod for capturing clips. All three devices also use their foldable nature to let you capture full-quality selfies using the rear cameras, which always have better quality than the 10MP dedicated front-facing cameras.

Here’s where specs on paper can tell a different story compared to real life.

The Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra may offer the biggest battery of the bunch at 5,000 mAh, but its battery life is on par with the smaller Galaxy Z Fold 8. Both foldables are designed to last you through a full day, though sticking to the outer display only on the Fold 8 Ultra may bring you slightly better battery life overall.

In either case, you’re also getting 45W fast charging over USB-C, granting you speedy recharging if your device happens to get low through the course of a busy day. These are also “Qi2 Ready,” meaning you can use magnetic wireless chargers if you use a compatible magnetic case.

One of the most important things you’ll want to ask yourself when buying a foldable phone is why you want to opt for a foldable in the first place, and each of these devices gives a different answer.

The Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra is a productivity powerhouse, delivering more space on its inner display for you to get work done through the web, apps, and more. You can run up to three apps at once on the Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra’s display, as well as floating apps, and more.

Galaxy Z Fold 8 is great for media consumption. The wider aspect ratio is perfect for watching videos, playing games, reading books, and more, all while still giving you plenty of space for productivity just like the Ultra.

Galaxy Z Flip 8, meanwhile, is all about giving you a more compact device. The outer display lets you quickly reply to messages or keep up with directions, while you can unfold it to do everything you want to on a normal phone. Close it up to more easily stow it away in your pocket or your bag.

If you are moving into a new Galaxy foldable, the good news is that you don’t have to start from scratch. Thanks to Samsung’s Smart Switch app, you can easily transfer supported content including pictures, contacts, messages, and more from an older Galaxy phone, another Android phone, or even an iPhone over to a Galaxy foldable. Apps, messages, photos, and more can all make the move wirelessly, without the need of a cable.

You can get the Galaxy Z Fold 8, Fold 8 Ultra, and Flip 8 from your favorite carriers and retailers, but Best Buy is one of the best places to score a great deal with solid trade-in values and deep discounts around Black Friday and throughout the holiday season.

She used eye tracking and gyroscopes for thumbstick control.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

EEG machines are used to detect and record brain activity, so one enterprising tech enthusiast decided to use them to play GTA VI. Creator Perri Karyal showed her feat on X, where she streamed her gameplay while wearing a wireless electroencephalogram (EEG) designed for research.

I STOLE A JET WITH MIND CONTROL IN GTA6 this took me AGES you have no idea what should I do next?? This electroencephalogram (EEG) records my brain activity and when I'm imagining something I've trained the software to recognise (like pushing a cube forward), a button will be pressed on my virtual xbox controller. For this run, I was imagining pushing a boulder to accelerate, getting angry to interact with vehicles, and imagining a cricket hopping to jump. I use eyetracking and gyro for joysticks :)November 22, 2026

Perri could be seen jumping a fence with Jason on a motorbike, before proceeding toward a parked fighter to steal and fly it. Her brain activity is overlaid on the screen while she’s playing, and viewers could also see the creator intensely focused on the game.

She trained the software to recognize specific brain signals and mapped them to an action on her Xbox controller. For example, imagining pushing a cube forward would correspond to a button press, getting angry lets her interact with cars and other vehicles, while picturing a hopping cricket would also make her character jump. The joysticks, on the other hand, are controlled via eye tracking and through gyroscopes on the head unit.

While Perri makes it look effortless, using the EEG device for gaming is way harder than what many people think. “People don’t realize how challenging it is!” Karyal replied to a comment. One viewer also mentioned the huge mass of brainwaves that appeared on the screen when she took off on the jet, to which she responded, “I was so stressed out. DO YOU KNOW HOW MANY TIMES I TRIED TO DO THIS.” Even though using wireless EEG for gaming does not have any apparent side effects, the creator said that she gets headaches after hours of gaming because of how much the headset squeezes her head.

This isn’t the first time that we’ve seen people playing games using just their mind. Neuralink has recently shown off one of its patients shooting at NPCs and even “threading the needle” with a speedboat using a brain-computer interface (BCI) chip. The big difference between Perri’s creation and Neuralink’s BCI, though, is that the latter requires invasive neurosurgery.

It may be surprising to think how a tech creator could beat Neuralink at its own game, but the latter has already applied the technology to multiple trial patients. We’ve seen them playing complicated games like World of Warcraft and Counter-Strike 2, while others used it for productivity and creativity, like editing a YouTube video.

These technologies are still in the development and trial phases or are being experimented on by enterprising creators and tech tubers. However, it also gives hope to individuals with limited mobility, as it gives them hope that they could regain some form of mobility and independence in the future.

#10657. Find Orphan Pages and Buried Content: Build an Internal Link Graph for Your Site

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The pages of a site form a graph. Think of it as many nodes connected to each other via edges. Each of the edges are the links between the pages, and the nodes themselves are the pages. Now if you start to visualize this as a graph, you will quickly see that there are multiple ways to traverse this graph and reach a particular endpoint.

A search crawler usually uses one of these pages as a starting point and crawls through all of the pages (especially when pages are not fully covered in a sitemap).

If a page lies very deep from the home page, it means that it's not an important page. It's an indication to the search engine that this is not something that you yourself consider important, and the search engine has a lesser incentive to rank this page over pages that are linked better within your site.

The key metric here is link depth. That is the number of clicks it takes to reach a particular page from your home page.

Another key metric is broken links. If you have too many broken links, it indicates to the search engine that your site is outdated and not well maintained, and the search engine will then not want to rank your site.

The way to identify both these problems is the same: to actually walk through your site, do a crawl of your site outside in, and try to figure out what the link depth is and if there are broken links on your website.

Seed the crawl with the home page and all URLs mentioned in the sitemap. Technically, the sitemap is not required if you're doing a crawl, but going from the sitemap will allow us to detect orphaned pages as well.

Fetch the pages and follow redirects as required. Record when you're following a redirect: we do not let fetch follow it silently, we record each hop and add its target to the crawl. Record HTTP errors as they occur.

Drop URL fragments and tracking parameters. Keep trailing slashes because sometimes there can be mismatches around expectation of trailing slashes in different URLs of a site.

In order to navigate from the home page, we can use BFS. So we know that the first time we reach a page, that is the page's shortest path.

We can cut off the depth over a certain threshold, say 5, to indicate that pages beyond that are too far or too deep to be reached.

Noindex pages will still be crawled. However, we need to make a record of them being not indexed, to reflect that they will not be present in the search engine.

The desired page depth is as close to the home page as possible. Important pages should be close to the home page. The deeper you go from the home page, it signals to the search engine that the page is less important.

A couple of examples of how a deep page is present:

The suggested fix for this is that we link the pages from as close to the home page as possible:

Wasted links are those internal links that do not do the job they're supposed to do. The different types of wasted links:

We have seen here how to look at our own website through the lens of a search engine. You can use some of the code over here to create your own crawler and identify internal linking issues; the full script is in this gist. Alternatively, we can use a free internal link visualizer to identify these issues and focus only on the fixes to improve our site's internal linking.

#10651. This Fitbit Air app buzzes your wrist with phone notifications, finds a lost tracker, more

Google’s Fitbit Air is a simple screenless fitness tracker, but it can do a bit more, and now someone has built an app that lets the Air buzz your wrist when phone notifications come through.

The era of vibe-coding has been very quickly bringing ideas to reality, and these new Fitbit Air notification apps are a great example.

“Air Relay” is the result of a developer managing to reverse-engineer a way to control haptics on the Fitbit Air via Bluetooth without affecting Google Health sync. “Cuff” uses the same method while also showing health stats if you don’t want to use Google Health.

The idea here is pretty simple. You can have apps on your phone ping your Fitbit Air as you choose. “Air Relay” lets you pick select apps and even change the vibration pattern or strength based on the app. You can set “Quiet hours” to prevent notifications while you’re sleeping, and there’s a WhatsApp-specific setting that lets you only get notifications from specific groups or people. A “Find My Air” setting uses Bluetooth strength you help you track down a lost tracker.

While a big part of the appeal of Fitbit Air is its screenless, disconnected nature, having the option to set up alerts for important apps or contacts is certainly a useful option.

“Air Relay” is free on Google Play, and Fitbit Air is available for $99.

#10648. Running an AI Agent on a Local LLM: What I Measured on One GPU

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Running an AI agent on your own GPU sounds simple: point it at Ollama and stop paying per token. I set up OpenClaw, an open-source AI assistant, with a 9B model in Ollama on one RTX 3090 and connected it to Discord. Then I read the Ollama log for every request.

The agent worked. The numbers were not what I expected, though. A one-line question turned into a prompt of more than 18,000 tokens, the first reply took over a minute, and the small model sometimes guessed instead of using its tools.

This article covers what I measured and what each number means if you plan to run an agent on local hardware.

OpenClaw ran in Docker and reached Ollama on the host at http://host.docker.internal:11434. Discord was the chat interface, and OpenClaw's web panel showed the sessions and context use.

This article is about the results, not the installation. The full step-by-step setup is in a separate guide to running OpenClaw in Docker with Ollama and Discord. That guide covers the Compose file, the Discord bot and how to keep the ports off your network.

All timings below come from the Ollama log on the test machine. Ollama loaded the model with a 32,768-token context.

My first message in Discord was short: "Which model are you running on, and what is 17 * 23?" The bot answered correctly: it ran on ollama/qwen3.5:9b, and 17 × 23 is 391.

The Ollama log showed what that question cost. The model processed 18,415 prompt tokens and generated 33 tokens.

The extra tokens come from the agent itself. With every request, OpenClaw sends:

On the RTX 3090, reading that prompt took 10 seconds at 1,838 tokens per second. A slower GPU, or a CPU, needs much longer to read the same prompt.

Prompt tokens per model call, from the Ollama log. Only the first message had to process the whole prompt.

OpenClaw counts tokens with its own estimate, which was higher than the Ollama count. The gateway log estimated the prompt at 24,608 tokens, against a prompt budget of 24,576 tokens for this model. By OpenClaw's own count, the base prompt alone used up the budget before any conversation history.

The web panel showed a similar figure: 68 to 69 percent of the context window was in use for the Discord sessions after only a few messages.

The first message arrived while the model was not loaded in GPU memory. That reply took 1 min 10 s:

Reply times on the test machine. Loading the model took most of the first reply.

The speed-up comes from prompt caching. The system prompt, tool definitions and workspace files at the start of the prompt stay the same between requests. Ollama kept that part cached and processed only the new tokens. The model wrote at 91 to 96 tokens per second in every test.

Ollama's documentation says models stay in memory for 5 minutes by default before they are unloaded. After that, the next message pays the load time again. You can change this with the OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE environment variable on the Ollama server, or with the keep_alive parameter per request. A negative value, such as -1, keeps the model loaded. I did not change this setting in the test.

If you run Ollama in Docker, pass the variable with -e OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE=-1 or in the environment: section of your Compose file.

Two things used the GPU besides my chat messages.

A heartbeat. OpenClaw's web panel listed one scheduled automation, heartbeat-main, which runs every 30 minutes. The agent wakes on that schedule, so the model gets called even when nobody chats. With Ollama's 5-minute default, the model is unloaded between heartbeats unless someone chatted recently, so a heartbeat most likely starts with a model load. I did not measure this.

An embedding model. During the first message, OpenClaw also called Ollama's /api/embed endpoint twice to index its memory. It used qwen3-embedding:4b, which was already installed on the Ollama server. I did not set OpenClaw to use that model; it picked it on its own.

The two models took this much GPU memory:

That is 11.4 GB for the models alone, almost half of the RTX 3090's 24 GB.

What this means for you: plan GPU memory for the chat model, the embedding model and the context, not the chat model alone. If the GPU also runs other models, the heartbeat can compete with them.

The first two tests in Discord: a question, then a file written with a tool. The user name is blurred.

The mistakes showed up when the model had to check something:

After the restart, the bot claimed it had lost the earlier session. When asked to use its read tool, it read the file correctly.

The fix for the first problem was simple. When I wrote "use your read tool to open homelab-test.md", the model opened the file and listed all three points correctly. Naming the tool made it check instead of guess.

The model listed four files and missed the test file and a folder, because it answered from its prompt.

Based on this test, a local 9B model on one consumer GPU works for:

If you need better answers, OpenClaw also works with hosted models, but then your prompts leave your machine.

In this test, qwen3.5:9b used 6.3 GB and the qwen3-embedding:4b memory model used 5.1 GB. Together the two models needed 11.4 GB.

The model had to load into GPU memory first, which took 59 seconds of the 70-second reply. Ollama keeps a model loaded for 5 minutes by default, and the OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE setting changes that.

Every request includes OpenClaw's system prompt, its tool definitions and its workspace files. A one-line question became an 18,415-token prompt in this test.

Yes. A heartbeat automation runs every 30 minutes and calls the model even when nobody sends a message.

It handled questions, arithmetic and file creation correctly in this test. It sometimes answered from its prompt instead of using its tools. When it wrongly said its memory was lost, asking it to use its read tool made it check the file and answer correctly.

#10647. Japan puts chipmaking labs inside trucks with mobileFab

Nipponese has semiconductor manufacturing on wheels is an interesting contrast to the U.S.' deployable Burger King trucks.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Multiple nations worldwide have chip-making programs in their respective universities, a discipline that is seeing increasing demand by the day. Japan is one such nation, and it currently has a program in which students can design circuits to wire chip wafers with miniaturized lithography stations. That gear is now going on wheels to schools and companies thanks to mobileFab trucks.

Each truck-mounted mobileFab container contains three machines: a photoresist coater, a maskless lithography machine, and a photoresist developer. Students take pre-etched wafers made by Japan's NIAIST (Institute for Advanced Industrial Science and Technology) and go through the necessary steps to wire the transistors therein together and produce a finalized chip core. The procedures are analogous to getting a breadboard with some pre-mounted, unconnected components and turning that into a product.

The three machines going into trucks are part of the larger Minimal Fab laboratories in place in some Japanese colleges. They're going on the move so that other schools and companies can train students/employees in the art of chipmaking. For now, the 12.5-mm wafers come from the NIAIST, but they can come from other foundries. Each machine is roughly 30 cm wide, and the machines use localized cleaning and sealed between-machine transportation so as not to have to rely on large-scale cleanrooms.

According to the press release by the Minimal Fab Promotion Organization, this system is not only meant for democratizing access to chip manufacturing education. The machines also allow for rapid prototyping and iteration of chip designs, and facilitate small-scale manufacturing — all with modest capital investment.

The "why" of this initiative is fairly predictable: the program's organizers predict that semiconductor fab engineers will be in high demand over the next 10 years, and there aren't even enough teachers going around, making systematic education difficult. Additionally, the division of labor in semiconductor fabs means that many engineers don't participate in all stages of the chip-making process, nor do they have opportunities to operate chip-making equipment themselves. The mobileFab will be on display at the SEMICON Japan 2026 conference in December.

#10640. Shared CXL memory pools come with steep software tax that could stall multi-server scaling

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The results land as memory makers push shared CXL pools as a home for AI’s KV cache.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Researchers at the University of Texas at Arlington and Intel measured what happens when four servers share one pool of CXL memory on real hardware. In a presentation shared at Supercomputer 2026 (SC26), they found that because the hardware doesn’t automatically keep shared data updated across servers, necessary software overhead adds a 3-5µ (microsecond) delay per lock, each time servers access the same data. These minute delays effectively cancel out the benefits — a finding that comes as CXL memory pools are being proposed as a potential AI KV cache solution.

Locks work like traffic signals for shared data: a server has to claim the lock before it changes something in the pool, and the other servers wait until it releases it. This way, no two servers overwrite the same data at the same time. Within a given server, the CPU’s hardware handles that handoff in a fraction of a microsecond, but across these servers, software is in charge of enforcement.

The setup comprises four 86-core Intel Xeon 6787P servers with 256GB of DRAM each, connected over PCIe 5.0 to one CXL memory box. The switch, which UT Arlington’s Peiran Du called “the only commercially available product we can use,” caps their setup at four servers. The catch is that there is no cross-host cache coherence or atomic operations on “a real four-host CXL shared-memory system.”

This means no synced CPU caches and no hardware atomics between servers to build locks on, so software has to handle both. Writers publish their cached copy out to the pool and wait for it to finish, while readers throw out stale copies before reading, with the locks built from plain reads and writes, such as Lamport’s fast mutex. CXL 3.0 defines a hardware fix for the caches, called Back-Invalidate, but the researchers hadn’t received vendor confirmation that their platform supports it.

Aside from the lock acquisition penalty, which is “roughly an order of magnitude higher than local hardware spin locks,” there is a visibility cost: “one-way cross-host visibility is about 1 µs.” This is 0.94 µs, one way, compared to 1.91 µs for an RDMA write on their 400Gb/s InfiniBand, so the link isn’t the cause. A local compare-and-swap takes about 100 ns and, across servers, a lock’s median is 3.7 µs if uncontended, but 132 µs at the 95th percentile with several servers on one lock.

The result: breadth-first search (BFS), a standard graph algorithm and a common benchmark, “gains nothing from adding a second host,” and was actually slower on four servers than on one, at 0.74s versus 0.55s. This was mostly not from the locks and barriers, which were only about 3.5% of its runtime, but due to the repeated flushing of shared state at every level.

On the other hand, throughput for a hash table — using independent buckets and per-host allocators — grew almost linearly when adding hosts. Triangle counting, another standard graph benchmark, also had a runtime reduction of more than half when scaling from one to four hosts.

Those costs matter, in part, because shared CXL pools are emerging as a new home for KV cache — an AI model’s stored record of a conversation’s state — once it outgrows GPU memory and a server’s own DRAM. The CXL Consortium’s SC26 session description says cloud providers and GPU makers “have highlighted CXL memory pooling as an effective approach for improving KV cache access.”

SK hynix, which makes CXL memory modules, showed a design that hands KV cache between GPU servers through a shared pool and claims 2.6x faster time to first token than standard RDMA setups. Its abstract also claims 3.0x lower P99 latency and 1.9x higher throughput. Micron, at SC26’s CXL Pavilion, showed its Abaco reference design of pooled CXL memory shared across rack servers and GPUs. It claimed 10x to 30x agentic AI gains, with famfs support for apps like Nvidia’s Dynamo.

The need is real, even if efficiency gains have led to less cache needed per token. Vikram Sharma Mailthody of Nvidia Research said at SC26’s Emerging Storage panel that his own analysis of open models on Hugging Face shows KV per token falling “by 50% every twelve months” with “up to 78 times reduction so far,” giving the example that 128K context took “about 64 gigabytes” three years ago and now takes less than one. But context lengths are heading toward millions of tokens, and “the product of those two will keep increasing,” he said.

Agents also add to the demand. Earlier in the conference, a workshop talk by Argonne National Laboratory and RIT researchers, based on public AI serving traces, showed that a “session calls again ~2 min later, once its tools return: a 160 GiB recency cache keeps 1% of its context,” needing the GPU to recompute the rest, or about 99%. Recomputation is significantly slower and more demanding, and memory is needed to cache more.

That makes it a capacity problem more than a speed problem. “Memory capacity is becoming a dominant factor. Memory latencies have not been seen as a primary problem yet,” Mailthody said. An agent’s context only has to survive a gap of a couple of minutes. It’s also ideal for any KV cache solution to be as inexpensive as possible, and given that a 128GB DIMM costs more than four times that of a 64GB DIMM last year. According to Intel’s Anil Godbole on SC26’s CXL panel, being able to add capacity past the DIMM slot limit — including reusing DDR4 — bolsters the case for CXL even further.

It doesn’t hurt that the KV cache handoff is write-once, read-many, which is the type of pattern that actually scaled in testing, although UT Arlington did not specifically test KV cache. Researchers behind OCEAN, a CXL emulator, backed this idea in the same paper session as UT Arlington, saying that software coherence is fine for read-heavy work, with hardware essential as writes climb. Its abstract reports that hardware coherence, at least in emulation, can give an 11x improvement at full coverage on Tigon database workloads.

Limitations still exist, which is why SK hynix, for one, is relying on “novel locking and coherence protocols” for its design. Hardware isn’t quite there yet, and Back-Invalidate might “be pretty costly,” Du said, suggesting that both hardware and software coherence may be required.

Micron, which built prototype KV cache sharing into Nvidia’s Dynamo, noted two primary failure cases in a CXL Consortium presentation last month: where the writer’s data never reaches the pool, and where the reader never sees the updates. The first can be fixed by producers flushing before blocks are published, so unflushed blocks can never be found. The second is fixed with a consumer flush, that is, invalidation of their cached lines, before reading. These are the fixes and costs that UT Arlington was measuring.

Micron cut its flushing cost by flushing each slot only once, which works because KV cache is content-addressed — a slot in the pool that hasn’t changed stays coherent after its first flush. Micron’s presentation also notes that neither failure causes a crash. Instead, the read returns plausible-looking data, and the output is wrong in subtle ways, with the wrong tokens on some requests under cache pressure.

Working against CXL as a solution is the fact that the memory pooling only reaches within a rack today, Godbole said; it doesn’t span a full data center. Marvell and Penguin Solutions, in collaboration and in addition to others like Lightelligence, have shown or announced memory expansion and pooling over optical links, which could stretch pooling past a single rack. CXL is also “not the protocol to ferry such big data,” Godbole added, as it’s built for smaller reads and writes, 64 bytes at a time, closer to how a CPU uses RAM. For bulk transfers, he said, RDMA is more efficient.

A research system called Beluga, in a paper published on arXiv a year ago, had KV cache in “a commercial CXL switch-based memory pool” with time to first token cut by 89.6% and throughput raised 7.35x compared with an RDMA-based setup once its cache was filled. This suggests some optimization could improve the usefulness of the technology, too.

CXL is clearly a hit or miss with how it is actually used being a main concern, with “a gap between the [CXL] spec and the implementation from different vendors,” according to UT Arlington’s Jia Rao. SK hynix’s second-generation CMM-DDR5, at 256GB and using CXL 3.2, has only been shown, and the device its KV cache design uses to select what to send is still what the company’s Younghoon Min called a “research prototype.” These are early times, and the enterprise landscape is simultaneously changing quickly.

On paper, shared CXL pools do fit the KV handoff idea, but until a commercial switch ships with confirmed coherence across servers, the best we have is KV cache numbers from vendor demos and research prototypes. Back-Invalidate should cut the software overhead UT Arlington measured, but Du expects it to add delays of its own; production systems would likely need a combination of hardware coherence and software approaches like Micron’s.

Shane Downing is a Contributing Writer for Tom’s Hardware, covering consumer storage, PC hardware, and AI.

#10638. OnePlus starts shutting down its website to ‘focus on customer support’

After shutting down its US operations earlier this year, OnePlus has quietly closed its website in some regions as it shifts to “focus on customer support.”

OnePlus announced in July that it would end new product launches in the US and Europe. Just weeks later, the OnePlus 15 and other products the brand was selling in the US were sold out for good and, more recently, OnePlus rolled out its Android 17 update to transition devices to Oppo’s ColorOS instead of OxygenOS.

Now, OnePlus has fully pulled the plug on its US website, as well as some other regions.

oneplus.com/us has purged all product listings, including for accessories, with a new message saying that the brand is “streamlining” the site:

Following our July 16, 2026 announcement, OnePlus is streamlining this website to focus on customer support for existing OnePlus devices. For warranty, repair, and other support, please visit our support site Click.

The same applies to the OnePlus website in the Canada, and other regions, though the UK and EU countries such as France still have an operational site that mostly push Oppo products over remaining OnePlus devices, a practice that started a few months ago prior to the shutdown. The OnePlus 15, for example, is no longer available to purchase in the UK at all. Presumably, though, the OnePlus website will shut down in these regions eventually as well.

The OnePlus website in India is unaffected and still selling devices.

The warranty website OnePlus links to appears to be more than a bit out of date, with a focus on 2023’s OnePlus Open. The most recent device mentioned is the OnePlus 13, though the page has been updated based on what we’re able to see on the Wayback Machine.

#10637. Best Ecommerce Platforms for Developers: Six Options and the Layers Each Leaves You

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Picking an ecommerce platform is really a decision about which layers you want to own: the storefront, the catalog and order logic, the payment flow, and the hosting underneath. Every platform below draws that line in a different place. If you have already settled the front-end question with a comparison like SitePoint's Astro vs Next.js guide, the next question is what sits behind it. Prices are from each vendor's pricing page as of November 2026.

Shopify is the fully managed option. Basic costs $29 a month with card rates from 2.9% plus 30 cents, and a 2% fee applies to orders processed through a third-party gateway on that plan. Developers get two routes: Liquid themes, or Hydrogen, Shopify's headless toolkit, where your framework owns routing and rendering and Hydrogen supplies the Storefront API client and cart. Templates exist for React Router on Oxygen and for Next.js on Vercel. Plus starts at $2,300 a month.

Whop is a business platform for taking payments and sending payouts, and it sits under a storefront you have already built rather than replacing it. The Checkout element runs in the browser with no server code and no API key on the page. You pass your own order ID as metadata, Whop copies it onto the payment, and your server fulfills from a signed webhook that follows the Standard Webhooks spec. Domestic cards cost 2.7% plus 30 cents with no monthly fee, buyers see payment methods chosen by their location across 195 countries, and the same balance pays out in over 200 countries, including to affiliates or sellers you onboard. Whop's own guide shows how to integrate a checkout API into a Next.js storefront end to end. The trade-off is that there is no catalog or shipping engine; you collect address fields at checkout and trigger fulfillment from the webhook.

WooCommerce is a free plugin on WordPress you host yourself, so you own updates, security, and performance. Its payment gateway, WooPayments, charges US merchants 2.9% plus 30 cents per card transaction, 1.5% more for international cards, 2% for currency conversion, and $25 per dispute. Everything is PHP hooks and filters, which makes it the natural pick for agencies already living in WordPress and the wrong pick for anyone who does not want to run a server.

BigCommerce hosts the back end and lets you bring any front end. Core is $29 a month billed annually, capped at $30,000 in trailing annual sales, and plans auto-upgrade as volume grows. There are no platform fees when you use an embedded payment provider, and a 2% fee on Core if you use another one. Catalyst, its reference storefront, is built on Next.js, React Server Components, and the Storefront GraphQL API, and deploys to Vercel.

Medusa is an open-source commerce framework in Node.js and TypeScript, with an MIT-licensed core and a commercial enterprise edition. SitePoint covered it when it was still a young Shopify alternative; it now has over 37,000 GitHub stars. Self-hosting is free. Medusa Cloud starts at $29 a month for development, $99 for launch, and $299 for scale, with no percentage of sales, and the company says you can export your data and self-host at any point.

Saleor is a GraphQL-native, API-only commerce platform written in Python and released under the BSD-3 license. Self-hosting is free. Saleor Cloud starts at $1,599 a month for up to $200,000 in monthly sales, with 0.8% on orders above that, and $3,999 a month at the next tier. It is built for teams with a dedicated engineering function and a large catalog, which is exactly who should pay that price.

Own only the storefront, and Shopify or BigCommerce will carry the rest. Own everything, and Medusa or Saleor give you a back end you can read. Already on WordPress, and WooCommerce is the obvious fit. Already have a storefront in Next.js or Astro and only need the money to move, and Whop is the shortest path. Whichever you pick, the checkout is where disputes start, so log what SitePoint's friendly fraud guide for developers recommends from day one.

#10635. I love YouTube on TV, but we desperately need better watchlist controls

Managing a YouTube watchlist shouldn’t be as frustrating as it is, especially on the most-used app in my life. Google’s premier streaming app has too many issues with resurfacing content; in my opinion, this is now a major problem that needs to be fixed.

Aside from my YouTube watchlist gripes, it’s no secret that moving from a web browser to the TV is ultimately still a frustrating experience.

Lots of things I love about watching videos on my laptop or PC just don’t translate well to when I’m sat on my sofa holding a remote trying to find something to watch after wrapping up for the day. One thing I’m getting annoyed with is how YouTube scales on TV and Android TV boxes because you’re given less choice and arguably poorer recommendations for content due to the constraints in place.

Understandably, you can’t compress the layout too much on a TV as you’re typically sat a few feet away and all aspects of the interface need to be legible. I would still like to tweak it, though, if that were possible.

Maybe we could get proper sections for content, like how Netflix and other streaming platforms offer categorized areas where you can search for “comedy” or “horror”. I’m almost certain that, with Google’s insane investment and massive AI push, where every video is already analyzed hundreds of times, that wouldn’t be too difficult to do.

The problem now is that the sidebar menu is just too broad. Gaming covers basically everything. Then each line of videos accounts for one title, but requires search and previous watch data to populate. I find myself often fighting with recommendations on my TV, whereas on desktop it’s not as much of a problem.

That leads me to my biggest problem with YouTube, and it’s the, frankly, embarrassing watchlist or “Watch later” controls.

I can’t fault that it works, but right now the watchlist function is like something out of the early 2000s due to the lack of flexibility that I touched on above. As my YouTube consumption has increased 20x over the past four or five years, managing what I want to come back to or might have missed the first time around is starting to become a bit of a chore.

I’ve tried using playlists to save things, but things get messy very quickly because of how cumbersome it is to a) create a playlist on TV and b) how playlists are organized by default.

Google TV isn’t perfect, but it’s where I keep my biggest TV and movie watchlist outside of YouTube. Yes, YouTube is mostly for user-generated content, but I would take something as basic as Google TV’s interface baked into YouTube somehow. Add in categorization and auto-clear.

The Watch Later section has become an unwieldy place that I never really want to go unless I desperately have to. I’m constantly losing videos that I hoped to come back to or have an affinity for.

Videos don’t clear automatically, nor do you know when they were added. Yes, I’ll give credit that you can sort by a few options, but that’s the bare minimum. The process of moving things around is slow and takes multiple taps. Watch Later is also hidden compared to other services. It’s under your account, then beneath “Liked videos,” which is below any you may have uploaded yourself.

On a TV, the process is so annoying I just avoid it altogether. You go to the sidebar menu > Library > Watch later. It should be front-and-center on a TV. It’s almost unforgivable in my eyes because even your watch history is given greater prominence. I simply cannot understand that decision.

I’m not always a huge fan of AI being shoehorned into apps and services, but this is one of those areas that has been left to rot for a while and could get a big uplift from adding in a little bit of Gemini. Even if it were just an enhanced search function or more tools for organizing.

With YouTube TV effectively taking over regular broadcast television and being the world’s biggest streaming platform, I’m shocked by how so many areas of the platform remain in a consistently neglected state.

A little bit of spit and polish, and maybe I’ll start using this section again. For now, though, I’ll stick to texting myself links of stuff I want to watch. The ball is in your court, Google.

#10630. Chess.com’s next move is a new hub for classic games, from mahjong to sudoku

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

AI changed the game at Chess.com, helping it expand to 11 other classics this year.﻿

AI changed the game at Chess.com, helping it expand to 11 other classics this year.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Chess.com is ready to go a lot bigger than chess. After launching Gambit, a gambling-free poker site, earlier this year, the company is now adding 10 more games: backgammon, checkers, go, hearts, mahjong, shogi, sudoku, solitaire, xiangqi, and a Scrabble alternative called word. They’re all part of Chess’s new Classic Games platform, which is launching its public beta on Saturday on the web and through a new app for iOS and Android.

“Our cofounders have had many people come up to them and say, ‘Hey, I run a mahjong night on Wednesdays’ or ‘I go to this local bar in Chicago with like five people, and we love playing go, but we lack a Chess.com,’” chief growth officer Albert Cheng says in an interview with The Verge. “The way that Chess.com has grown this community of almost 300 million members has not really happened for a lot of these other types of classic games.”

Classic Games is aimed at filling that gap. Some of the new games, like shoji or xiangqi, are strategy games similar to chess while others are further afield, like sudoku, solitaire, and word. The Classic team chose the new games through “a mix of community feedback, market research, and then just our own confidence in our ability to serve that game well,” Cheng says. It’s focused on classics that “have some sort of lifelong journey of learning and a community.”

Classic Games will feel familiar if you’ve ever played a match on Chess.com. Each game has lessons, leaderboards, and a rating system. Multiplayer games like checkers or mahjong have options for matches against other players or bots, and they use the same bot characters that Chess.com has. (Martin remains the lowest-rated bot across the board, whether it’s on Chess, Gambit, or Classic.) Not all of the games have the same play modes — word and mahjong have puzzles like chess, while sudoku has a set of daily grids and solitaire has daily deals.

The expansion hasn’t put Chess.com itself on the back burner, according to Cheng. If anything, it’s never been better. “Chess is more and more so in the public zeitgeist than I’ve ever seen it, frankly, during my time here,” Cheng says. “Our daily active users are higher than they’ve ever been during the company’s history.” He declined to share exactly how many daily users Chess.com currently has but said the site is “doing amazingly well.”

So, while the company is expanding beyond its domain name, chess remains its primary focus. “The amount of folks that are fully dedicated toward building new games is less than 1 percent of the company,” according to Cheng.

AI played a key role in helping Chess.com launch 11 new games in a matter of months with a team of only a few people. Earlier this year, Cheng told The Verge his team built Gambit with the help of AI, mainly for coding tasks. He says they used “almost entirely the same approach” for Classic, with AI helping to speed up the coding process again.

“Because these games are built on top of the Chess.com code base and design system and bot characters and rating systems, it’s a huge advantage and allows us to move very quickly alongside AI to bring these types of games to market with a very small team,” Cheng says. “As we create more and more games, the adjacent games that can be created to it just happen much faster because the infrastructure is there, as opposed to standing everything up from scratch.”

AI wasn’t used for Classic Games’ art and design, Cheng says. “The look and feel of it is very carefully crafted by humans, and we’re proud of that,” he says. “The truth is, it really is a blend. It’s a small team of really talented folks that have good product and design judgment, and they’re leveraging AI.”

“We are fortunate in some ways that AI and chess have been intermixed for 30-ish years.”

Chess.com’s comfort with AI may come from the fact that human chess players have now spent decades playing and learning alongside machines. “We are fortunate in some ways that AI and chess have been intermixed for 30-ish years,” Cheng says, calling back to Garry Kasparov’s 1996 chess match against IBM’s Deep Blue supercomputer. “Even before the company was founded 20 years ago, the idea that AI was super powerful at the game of chess was embedded in how the cofounders thought about the company.”

Elsewhere in the games industry, there’s been increasing tension over the use of AI. Cheng says that while Chess.com may be using AI to “accelerate” coding tasks, the creative side of things has become more important. “On the art and the design side, the ratios are shifting a decent amount, where we’re hiring more of those folks,” he says, adding that the company has “shipped a lot more major features in Chess.com than in previous years” using what they’ve learned from Classic Games’ faster development process.

Chess.com might not be done growing its library of games, either. Cheng said it will likely expand to “a lot more” games in the future.
