#10582. Which 2026 Samsung Galaxy Tab should you buy?

Samsung has a huge lineup of Galaxy tablets to choose from right now, and they’re all a little different.

The Galaxy Tab S12+ is the Samsung tablet to choose for a premium mix of entertainment, creativity, and productivity. The Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra gives you an even bigger canvas, while the Galaxy Tab S11 brings flagship features to a more portable size. For everyday value, start with the Galaxy Tab S10 FE series or the affordable Galaxy Tab A11+.

Samsung’s tablet lineup covers everything from watching a show on the couch to sketching your next project or getting work done between meetings. The right choice comes down to what you want that extra screen to do.

With options across a wide range of price points, Samsung has a Galaxy tablet for different needs and budgets this holiday season.

Here’s a list of the main Galaxy Tab options available in 2026, from family-friendly entertainment to Samsung’s largest creative workspace:

Screen size is only one part of the whole story. The Galaxy Tab S11, S12+, and S12 Ultra have AMOLED displays that offer rich contrast for movies and photos, while the FE models provide useful everyday features at a more accessible price.

The Galaxy Tab S12+ is the sweet spot if you want a serious upgrade over a basic tablet without moving to Samsung’s biggest screen.

As we covered in our Galaxy Tab S12 launch story, it combines a 12.6-inch 120Hz AMOLED display with a MediaTek Dimensity 9500 processor, 12GB of RAM, and an included S Pen, with four months of complimentary Adobe Photoshop access and a six-month Notability trial, both of which support the stylus.

The S12+ is easy to use and appreciate in daily life. It puts reference material beside your notes during a course, sketch ideas for a presentation, while making it easy to settle into a movie after finishing your email. It has room to spread out without requiring the Ultra’s larger footprint.

The Galaxy Tab S12+ also offers a 5G version, making it worth considering if you regularly work away from Wi-Fi.

Buy the Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra when the screen itself is the upgrade you’ll enjoy every day.

The Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra is made for creative professionals and families seeking premium entertainment. It moves naturally between focused creative work and immersive movie nights at home.

Its 14.6-inch AMOLED display gives photos, drawings, and multiple app windows more breathing room. Picture reviewing a document while keeping your handwritten notes visible, or drawing with reference images alongside your canvas.

The Ultra shares the S12+’s Dimensity 9500 processor but offers configurations up to 16GB of RAM and 1TB of storage. It’s available for $1,399.99, but last year’s Tab S11 Ultra is on sale during Black Friday for just $899.99.

Both S12 tablets ship with Android 17-based One UI 9 and seven years of OS and security updates. Photoshop comes pre-installed with a four-month access offer, giving you a way to explore S Pen-assisted photo editing.

The Ultra is especially appealing with a keyboard at a desk; Samsung’s Ultra Book Cover Keyboard Slim is the perfect companion to the S12 Ultra, and is on sale for $146.99 during Best Buy’s Black Friday Deal event.

Yes, the Galaxy Tab S11 is the premium Galaxy tablet to choose when portability comes first.

Our Galaxy Tab S11 launch coverage highlighted the return of Samsung’s smaller flagship tablet, with a Dimensity 9400+ processor, 12GB of RAM, and an included S Pen. Its 11-inch AMOLED screen makes it an attractive companion for reading, travel, and handwritten notes.

The Galaxy Tab S11 is the sort of tablet you can bring to a meeting or pull out on a train without needing a large work surface. You still get premium display quality, just in a size that’s easier to carry, and it comes in at a lower price.

A discounted Tab S11 Ultra can also be worth considering if you want a 14.6-inch screen. Compare the actual price with the S12 Ultra rather than dismissing an older flagship automatically.

The Galaxy Tab S10 FE and FE+ are compelling choices for people who want an included S Pen without flagship pricing.

Choose the 10.9-inch FE for a study companion that travels easily. Choose the 13.1-inch FE+ when you’d rather put a lecture, document, or recipe beside your notes. Both offer IP68 water and dust resistance, so they’re great kitchen companions.

Galaxy Tab S10 Lite is for students, children, and emerging creators. It is a practical, encouraging gift for handwritten learning, organization, drawing, and experimentation.

The Galaxy Tab A11+ is the straightforward entertainment choice. At $199, it’s the cheapest of the Galaxy Tab options, but it doesn’t support the S Pen, so choose an S-series model if handwriting matters.

Choose the Galaxy Tab S12+ for a premium all-rounder, the S12 Ultra for a spacious creative workspace, or the S11 for flagship features in a travel-friendly size.

For school, home projects, and everyday notes, the FE series delivers the features many people actually need. For family streaming and browsing, the A11+ keeps things simple and affordable.

The best Galaxy Tab isn’t automatically the biggest or newest. It’s the one that makes the things you already love doing easier — and gives you a few new possibilities along the way.

#10577. Markdown to PDF in 2026: No-Install Methods and the Rendering Problems Nobody Warns You About

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Converting Markdown to PDF is easy until the document has real content: long code blocks, wide tables, images and non-Latin text are where most tools fall apart.

Pandoc with LaTeX is still the most powerful route, but it needs a large install and trips over Unicode and emoji unless you switch engines and fonts.

Browser-based approaches (print CSS, Chromium-powered converters, in-browser tools) avoid the install and give you HTML-quality rendering, as long as you control page breaks.

A handful of print CSS rules (@page, break-inside: avoid, break-after: avoid) fix the majority of ugly PDFs, whichever tool you use.

Choose the tool by the document: a quick README export, a long technical report and a multilingual document each have a different best option.

I write almost everything in Markdown: READMEs, release notes, client handovers, meeting notes. And at least once a week, someone asks for "the PDF version". Every time, the conversion looks fine on the first page and falls apart somewhere around page three: a code block split in half, a table running off the edge, an emoji replaced by an empty box.

SitePoint covered the classic route back in 2015 with Creating PDFs from Markdown with Pandoc and LaTeX. That approach still works. But a lot has changed since then, and today there are several ways to get a clean PDF without installing a TeX distribution. This article compares them, then goes through the rendering problems you'll actually hit, with a fix for each.

Pandoc is still the Swiss Army knife. The simplest command is:

By default, that goes through LaTeX, which means you need a TeX distribution installed (several gigabytes for a full one). The output is beautiful for prose and math, but LaTeX has strong opinions about layout, and customizing it means writing LaTeX.

Pandoc can also render through an HTML engine instead, which lets you style the PDF with CSS:

Best for: long documents, academic writing, math, automated pipelines.

Watch out for: install size, Unicode handling with the default engine (more on that below).

Render the Markdown to HTML (any previewer, a static site, a GitHub page) and print it to PDF. It's free and already installed everywhere, but you inherit whatever the page looks like on screen: navigation bars, sidebars, and page breaks wherever they happen to fall.

Best for: quick, one-off exports of short documents.

Watch out for: zero control over page breaks unless the page ships print CSS.

Extensions like Markdown PDF for VS Code, or CLI tools like md-to-pdf, render your Markdown to HTML and print it with a headless Chromium:

You get the same rendering quality as a browser, plus a config file for CSS, margins, headers and footers. The trade-off is a Node install and a downloaded Chromium.

Best for: developers who live in their editor or want CI-friendly exports.

Watch out for: the first run downloads a browser, and print CSS is still your job.

The newest option is tools that do the whole conversion inside the browser tab: you paste or drop the .md file, preview the actual pages and download the PDF. The good ones don't upload your file anywhere, which matters for client documents and internal notes.

Best for: people who don't want to install anything, non-developers on your team, private documents.

Watch out for: many online converters do upload your file to a server; check before you paste anything confidential.

Whichever method you pick, the same handful of problems show up as soon as the document is longer than a page. Most of them are fixed with print CSS, which works in Pandoc's HTML engines, Chromium-based tools and any HTML you print from a browser.

A 20-line function cut after line 3, with the rest on the next page, is the most common complaint. The fix is to ask the renderer not to break inside the block:

One caveat: a block longer than a full page has to break. For those, it's better to let it break cleanly than to push it to a new page and leave a huge gap.

On screen, a long line in a code block scrolls horizontally. On paper, there's no scrollbar, so it's simply cut off. Wrap long lines in print:

Tables with many columns overflow the page or get squeezed until every cell is one word per line. Three things help: a smaller font size for tables, a landscape page for the worst offenders, and avoiding breaks in the middle of short tables:

If a table genuinely needs eight columns, consider whether it should be a table in the PDF at all, or an appendix in landscape.

A heading alone at the bottom of a page, with its content on the next one, looks broken. This rule keeps a heading attached to what follows it:

Support for break-after: avoid varies between rendering engines, so test it with yours; when it isn't honored, a manual page break before the heading is the reliable fallback.

Markdown has no syntax for a page break. The usual trick is a raw HTML element that print CSS turns into a break:

Some converters also recognize a comment like , which has the advantage of staying invisible when the same file is rendered on GitHub.

Images are inserted at their natural size, so a 4000-pixel screenshot spills off the page. Cap them:

For blurry images, the problem is usually the opposite: a small screenshot stretched to page width. Export screenshots at 2x if they're going into a PDF.

This one is specific to the LaTeX route. With the default pdflatex engine, Unicode characters outside its supported set produce errors or simply vanish. Switch to XeLaTeX or LuaLaTeX and give it a font that actually contains the characters:

Emoji are harder still in LaTeX and often need a dedicated package. HTML-based renderers handle both far better, because they use the operating system's font fallback just like a web page does.

Internal links ([see setup](#setup)) and external links should stay clickable. HTML-based renderers usually keep both. If yours doesn't, check that headings get IDs (most Markdown parsers add them) and that the PDF engine preserves link annotations.

Put together, these rules make a solid starting point for any HTML-based route:

Save it as print.css and pass it to Pandoc (--css=print.css with an HTML engine), to md-to-pdf through its config, or include it in the HTML you print from the browser.

My rule of thumb after a lot of trial and error:

A quick README or notes export, no install, private content: an in-browser converter. Full disclosure: this need is why I built the free Markdown to PDF converter on AwesomeToolkit, and it's what I use for this case now. It renders real A4 or Letter pages as you type, has an option to keep code blocks and tables whole, adds page numbers and a table of contents, and the conversion happens in the browser, so the file isn't uploaded anywhere.

Documentation in a repo, exported in CI: md-to-pdf or Pandoc with an HTML engine, plus the stylesheet above.

Long reports, math, academic formatting: Pandoc with XeLaTeX, using a font that covers every script in the document.

Something you'll print once and forget: the browser's Print to PDF is fine.

Whatever you choose, the biggest quality jump comes from the print rules, not the tool. Ten lines of CSS turn "it looks fine until page three" into a PDF you can actually send.

Yes. Your browser's Print to PDF works on any rendered Markdown, and in-browser converters do the whole conversion in a tab. Check that the converter processes files locally if the content is private.

The default pdflatex engine has limited Unicode support. Use --pdf-engine=xelatex or lualatex and set a font that contains the characters with -V mainfont= (and -V CJKmainfont= for CJK text).

Markdown has no native syntax for it. Insert

where you want the break, or use the page-break comment your converter supports.

Add pre { break-inside: avoid; } to your print CSS. Blocks longer than a page will still break, which is expected.

With HTML-based renderers, usually yes, both external links and links to headings inside the document. Test internal links in particular, since they depend on headings having IDs.

#10574. Claude rolling out updated Android app with Material 3 Expressive redesign

Anthropic is rolling out a new Claude app for Android that picks up a Material 3 Expressive redesign.

Confirmed on Twitter/X by Anthropic Product Manager Robert Bye, Claude is now rolling out a Material 3 Expressive redesign for its Android app that, while not a full embrace of Google’s design language, does make a clear attempt to better fit in on Android.

The updated design picks up a bottom-bar UI as opposed to relying fully on the old hamburger menu – it still exists, but it’s mainly for chat history now. You can see M3 elements throughout the app, including in the model switcher and the background of chats. It’s clearly themed to match Anthropic’s aesthetic, and there’s no obvious implementation of Material You colors, but it all fits within the general Material 3 guidelines – much more so than the original app.

The one flaw we spotted was the predictive back implementation, which gets a neat animation to move the search bar but is supposed to show a preview of the previous screen, but that’s just nit-picking, really.

The new Claude app for Android is rolling out now.

#10572. Hands-on: Baseus’ new PicoGo Pro USB-C charger is clever, cute, and on sale

USB-C chargers are pretty standardized at this point, but there’s a fun new trend of adding displays to your power brick that I’ve been very much enjoying, and the new PicoGo Pro from Baseus is a great example with three ports, optimized charging, and a cute little character to top it off – and for Black Friday, it’s pretty deeply discounted, too.

The Baseus PicoGo Pro (AH22) is a 100W, 3-port USB-C charger designed to top off your laptop, phone, and/or tablet, even all at the same time. For anyone who travels often or just has a lot of devices on hand, chargers like this are a must. Having something you don’t have to think twice about when it comes to charging rates is just so nice, and these have come a long way, too.

Using GaN tech, PicoGo Pro is very compact at about 2 inches tall and a little over an inch wide. I remember having single-port chargers that could barely fast-charge my phone that were twice this size just a few years ago. GaN just absolutely rules, and Baseus is using a special version that reduces heat and improves efficiency.

But the two reasons I really like this charger aren’t strictly to do with GaN.

The first is the built-in display. Again, that’s a trend throughout the industry. Anker has several chargers out with built-in displays as the company helped to really make this popular – the Anker Prime 160W brick is really similar to PicoGo Pro (at a higher price), and it’s the only I’ve been using for months before trying out this Baseus model.

Baseus has a cute little animated character that accompanies your charging, with little eyes when the charger isn’t active and some fun animations to show your charging rate in real-time. Neat! You can “press” the button to move through the UI and even see the specific charging rate of each connected device. You’ll also see that the brick can recognize the device you have plugged in, though Android devices just show a generic “SAMSUNG” or whatever brand you plug in – Anker, again, had a similar set of features in a brick released earlier in 2026, but some of those features were iPhone-only, and that brick was only a 1-port, 45W charger anyway.

But, like that Anker brick, Baseus supports “smart” charging that adjusts to your device. Plugging in a Galaxy Z Fold 8 that was around 65% full, I saw the brick adjust its charging speed to tailor to the phone, initially going up to full speed before settling down around 28W~. You can manually adjust this with “Turbo” or “Gentle” modes, the former maxing out the charging rate and the latter settling in around 15W for a slower charge. That flexibility is great because it means you can adjust the charging rate to what you need in that moment. Full-speed for a quick top-off on a busy day, slower charging overnight to preserve battery health.

Baseus isn’t doing much new here, though the GaN tech mentioned earlier is enhanced by some architecture and algorithm adjustments the company has made behind the scenes, but it’s a really good implementation.

Beyond that, it’s also priced pretty well.

Baseus PicoGo Pro’s full price is $59.99, but you’ll probably rarely see it there. Technically, today is its launch date, and it’s already down by over 30% at $39.70 for Black Friday. Value-wise, this one is hard to beat.

The latest in 3D printing tech comes to Frankfurt.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Once a year, all the world’s 3D printing manufacturers gather for Formnext in Frankfurt, Germany, to showcase new technologies, software, and ideas. This year’s show was more compact, with three halls instead of four, but consumer 3D printers had a larger presence. Spread across two halls, we found Bambu Lab, Prusa Research, Snapmaker, Creality, Elegoo, Anycubic, and Flashforge, plus a few newbies like Axiloop.

Startup SnowX was a popular booth, presenting the first consumer-grade desktop metal 3D printer coming soon to Kickstarter.

I counted ten tool-changing 3D printers on the show floor, five of which I’ve already reviewed. Elegoo surprised us all by launching its Centauri X Ultra four-color tool changer at the show, while Axiloop’s MC 8 arrived on my front porch that morning.

Only Anycubic is standing pat with a single-nozzle multicolor machine. Instead, it presented its new partnership with Thingiverse.

Full-color UV printers were on display as well, with Flashforge showing off its CJ270, which was teased last year.

Editor’s Note: Snapmaker provided travel accommodations for this trip. Coverage remains editorially independent.

Bambu Lab showed off its latest printers, the H3C, a tool changer (starting at $2,499) with nine lightweight tools we reviewed earlier in the month, and its first laser, the R1, reviewed in September, which retails at $2,499.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

More interesting is the launch of “Bambu Circle,” a pilot program that will start in the Netherlands and slowly expand across Europe. The goal is a network of local hubs to assist entrepreneurs with their 3D printing side hustle. Bambu Circle would enlist repair specialists and community ambassadors to provide a helping hand and an in-person retail shop with space for meetups. ,

At the moment, there is no plan to launch a similar program in the United States; however, we have many retailers here who offer official warranty support, parts, and tons of filament. A map on the Bambu Lab lists 31 US resellers, though that number is a bit skewed since there are also 31 MicroCenters in the US.

Prusa Research previewed the Rugged, a CORE One L+ adapted for printing in the harshest environments. The printer has a sealed case that keeps out both humidity and dust while providing extra protection during transport for machines used in the field. The CORE One L+ tucked inside is equipped with a high-temperature, hardened steel nozzle for printing advanced engineering filaments. It also comes with Prusa’s Zero-Trace system, which is an air-gapped machine with no flash drive storage, no USB slot, no Wi-Fi, and no camera. They can only accept files from your own cryptographically paired Prusa Connect server. These extremely secure, extremely tough machines are meant for classified and proprietary work environments.

However, you don’t need to have a security clearance to jump on board Prusa Connect Local, a free-to-use version of Prusa Connect for anyone who wishes to keep their 3D printers off the internet. The server can run on Linux, Windows, Mac, or a Raspberry Pi, and can handle a print farm of two to 400. It’s currently set up for any Prusa Printer, but it is open source, so you are free to tinker around with it and add your own extensions.

Snapmaker showed off what you can do with its four-color U1 tool changer. Ratdoux, the developer behind Full Spectrum, was on hand to chat with us. He devised a system of color-blending filaments that take thin layers of cyan, yellow, and magenta mixed with either gray or white, which can create nearly any other color. He said it was inspired by a conversation on Reddit where users were mixing colors in Blender, and he came up with a simpler way to do it in the slicer. Snapmaker immediately hired him to lead a development team for its fork of OrcaSlicer.

Amazingly, Full Spectrum went from an experimental slicer hack to a must-have for every tool changer in the space of nine months. Snapmaker’s Full Spectrum is tailored for the U1 on open-source OrcaSlicer, while Bambu Lab installed a “Color Mixing” tool in Bambu Studio and Prusa Research developed their own “ColorMix” technology based on hex codes.

Full Spectrum and similar tools can be used on any multimaterial 3D printer. However, because it looks best with super thin layers, tool changers are more efficient and less wasteful.

Snapmaker has since introduced a Full Spectrum filament bundle optimized for four-color machines: a CMYW for lighter, bright colors and a CMYG (gray edition) for darker shades. Ratdoux said the secret is in the filament’s light Transmission Distance (TD), which measures how translucent the material is. Colors that allow more light to pass through them create a better illusion of blending. The parrot and fox pictured above were printed using CMYG and a .08mm layer height. There was no orange, purple, green or black filament used in those prints.

We also found Axiloop, an eight-color tool changer that’s already raised over $700,000 on Kickstarter. The fully enclosed CoreXY machine has a 300x300mm build plate, 4-second tool swaps, and claims to have “near-zero” wipe-free tool changes. Its price for the MC8 Pro is $1,499 for early backers, while the open frame MC8 is $1,099.

Our review unit arrived while I was at Formnext, so I’m excited to put it to the test soon.

Peopoly’s Giga 800 is a large-scale pellet feeder that’s a bit north of our normal prosumer range at $15,000, but I felt it was worth mentioning because it's from our friends at Siraya Tech, who make superior filaments and resin. I noticed quite a few YouTubers happily climb into the machine to show how big it truly is.

The machine has an extrusion output of three kilos an HOUR, which would be equivalent to three spools of normal filament, and can use a 3mm nozzle for chonky prints. We’ve reviewed a similar-sized open-frame “giga” machine before, so I know that being hampered by a normal .4mm nozzle is not something you want with a print volume this large. The enclosed machine is capable of running everything from PLA and TPU to PET-CF and PPA-GF.

Creality had quite a few machines out, but the most interesting were the Creality Falcon T1 UV Laser we reviewed this summer and the new Falcon A1C portable laser we’ll be reviewing soon. The T1 is able to do inner engraving in glass in just a few minutes, and the team was happy to show me the workflow after I supplied them with a photo. They took my selfie, ran it through Creality’s AI to create a 3D bust, then zapped it into a 2 x 3 inch chunk of glass. It was an impressive demo.

They also showed off several handheld scanners, which Creality feels will enable makers to create more practical prints without needing a ton of CAD skills. My favorite is the Creality Pika we reviewed in October.

I’m also particularly intrigued by the mBox kid printer, with a small 153x153mm build plate but lots of safety features to make it child-friendly. The printer is priced at $329, which isn’t bad for a full-feature printer. However, good luck finding one to buy: this printer is only available on the TikTok Shop in the US until December 10.

Another disappointment was the Creality M1 filament maker with its companion R1 shredder. The unit was on display, but not running, which makes me believe they haven’t quite figured out how to make the dream of home filament making a reality.

Elegoo surprised us all with the Centauri X Ultra, a four-color tool changer variation of the wildly successful Centauri Carbon. The Centauri X Ultra is scheduled to release early in 2027. It has a 256x256mm bed, making it the same size as its siblings. The machine is completely enclosed and cleverly contains the side-mounted spools in a Tupperware-like sealed box. The machine has filament drying and a nozzle that can hit 350C, making it a good candidate for high-temperature filaments. It also claims to have 5-second color swaps without waste, but we’ll test that as soon as we get our hands on one.

FlashForge revealed its CJ270 full-color 3D printer, priced at $ 3,999 and now shipping. It is listed as a MultiJet Printer, which uses UV ink to build up models, with a build size of 180 x 120 x 100 mm. Unlike other UV printers, the CJ270 has seven printheads (one for each ink channel), so if one gets clogged, you don’t need to replace the entire printhead. It uses CMYK ink, plus white, clear, and a water-washable support material. The sample models shown were impressive, with lots of detail and strong color.

Anycubic didn’t have any new machines to show off, but they did have a huge announcement to make: they are collaborating with Thingiverse to bring millions of models into Anycubic’s file-sharing library, MakerOnline. Select designs will be converted into multicolor models for one-click printing from Anycubic’s mobile app.

The company is also looking into adding an AI tool dubbed AnyLab to its website to help users create their own designs.

#10569. What Is the Best UI design Tool? 8 Tools Compared by the Job They Do (2026)

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The ideal UI testing tool is the one that discovers what your users discover first: broken layouts, usability issues that no longer work, and new accessibility issues in all the browsers your users use before a change goes into the repository. In 2026, most frontend teams don't rely on a single tool to do all of that. The best setups involve two layers: a test runner layer that launches the browser and monitors behavior, and a UI testing and review platform that visually checks the outcome and routes changes to the right stakeholders for sign-off.

Even with the right stack, it still relies on what your team needs to catch. Different teams will focus on different points: a QA team trying to automate checkout flows, a design system team defending shared components, and a single developer working on a tight budget will all be somewhere different. This guide is categorized by the job these UI testing tools do, then connects them.

Most "best UI testing tool" lists rank test runners and visual testing platforms against each other as if they were interchangeable. They aren't. UI testing covers four distinct jobs:

Functional testing: Does the UI respond correctly when a user clicks, types, or hovers?

Visual testing: Does the UI still look right across layout, color, typography, and spacing?

Accessibility testing: Can people using keyboards and screen readers use it, and did this change introduce new violations?

Cross-browser and device coverage: Does it work in Safari on mobile, not just Chrome on a developer's laptop?

There are also other levels of UI tests. Unit tests test the logic but don't interact with the UI. End-to-end (E2E) tests represent the full user journey in a browser but are difficult to set up, hard to troubleshoot when the bug is a visual detail, and can't practically test every state. Between the extremes of unit tests and E2E tests, component tests simulate each component's behavior in a real browser using fixed inputs, offering speed closer to unit tests and user-level coverage closer to E2E. Many teams maintain a thin layer of E2E tests for critical journeys, and they have component tests for the dozens of states a component may be in.

These tools are organized by job so you can stack them rather than choose one winner

These platforms sit on top of your test runner. They capture rendered UI, compare it to approved baselines, and give your team a place to review what changed.

Chromatic is a UI testing and review platform made by the Storybook team. It catches visual, interaction, and accessibility bugs in components and pages before they merge, and it gives developers, designers, and product managers one place to review UI changes and sign off. Chromatic's team is the core maintainer of Storybook, which has about 90,000 GitHub stars and 17 million weekly npm downloads, and a Vitest core maintainer on the team built Chromatic's Vitest integration. Chromatic is used by 45 Fortune 100 companies, including GitHub, Shopify, Microsoft, Adobe, the BBC, and monday.com.

Pricing: Free plan with 5,000 snapshots per month (Chrome only), unlimited projects and users. Starter is $179/month for 35,000 snapshots and adds Safari, Firefox, and Edge. Pro is $399/month for 85,000 snapshots. Enterprise is custom, with SSO, SCIM, SLA support, and data retention. TurboSnap snapshots count as 0.2 of a regular snapshot.

Trade-offs: Chromatic is strongest when your UI is built from components. Teams with no component tests can start from Playwright or Cypress E2E tests, but they'll get the most value once components are covered too. Snapshot usage also grows with each browser and viewport you add, so plan coverage before you scale it.

Best for: Design system teams, product teams that want designers and PMs to approve UI changes, React Native teams, and Vitest-first teams that want visual testing without baselines stored in the repo.

Percy is BrowserStack's visual testing platform. It captures screenshots from your existing tests, renders them across browsers and responsive widths, and highlights pixel differences for review.

Strengths: Framework-agnostic SDKs for Playwright, Cypress, Selenium, and Storybook. App Percy covers native mobile apps. The free plan includes 5,000 screenshots per month with unlimited users and projects.

Trade-offs: Percy is page-oriented, and each page rendered in each browser at each width counts as a separate screenshot, so usage climbs quickly with cross-browser coverage. Percy compares feature branches against a base branch, which teams with complex branching should test early. Reviewers see rendered screenshots rather than an inspectable copy of the code under test, so debugging a diff usually means reproducing it locally.

Pricing: Free tier, then paid plans with fixed screenshot allowances and overage.

Best for: Teams already paying for BrowserStack that want visual checks on page-level E2E tests.

Applitools Eyes uses Visual AI to compare screenshots the way a person would, with match levels that can ignore content changes and focus on layout. Its Ultrafast Grid re-renders captured page states across many browser, viewport, and device combinations.

Strengths: Mature AI matching, broad browser and device coverage, Figma and Storybook testing, accessibility testing, and an MCP server. Higher tiers add AI-driven functional testing and plain-English test authoring.

Trade-offs: Applitools is priced and packaged for enterprises. There's a free trial but no free plan, and the Starter plan is $667/month billed annually for 100,000 component checkpoints or 1,000 page checkpoints. Professional and Enterprise are custom-priced.

Best for: Large QA organizations with big cross-browser matrices and budget for a dedicated visual AI platform.

BackstopJS is a free, open-source visual regression testing tool released under the MIT license. You list the URLs, viewports, and CSS selectors to capture in a config file, and BackstopJS renders them in a headless browser through Playwright or Puppeteer, then compares the results against approved reference screenshots. The core workflow runs on three commands: backstop init, backstop test, and backstop approve.

Strengths: Completely free, with no screenshot limits. A built-in browser report shows reference and test images side by side with a visual diff inspector, and changes can be approved from there. Optional Docker rendering keeps screenshots consistent between laptops and CI; Playwright adds Firefox and WebKit, and click, hover, and keypress selectors capture interactive states. JUnit reports plug into existing CI pipelines.

Trade-offs: BackstopJS is self-hosted, so there's no cloud dashboard, shared team review, or pull request status checks out of the box. Reference screenshots are usually committed to your repository, and every page or component state you want to test needs its own URL and scenario in the config. Someone on the team also has to own the setup, CI integration, and maintenance.

Best for: Developers and small teams with no budget who are comfortable running and maintaining their own visual regression setup.

Test runners click, type, and assert. They're free and open source, and every platform above builds on them. On their own, they offer limited visual testing: built-in screenshot assertions store baselines in your repository, rendering can drift between laptops and CI, and designers have no review interface.

Playwright is Microsoft's open-source browser automation framework. It runs tests across Chromium, Firefox, and WebKit, with auto-waiting, trace viewer debugging and code generation, and supports JavaScript, TypeScript, Python, Java, and .NET.

Visual testing: Playwright's toHaveScreenshot assertion compares screenshots against baselines stored in Git. It works, but baselines must be regenerated per operating system, and accepting a change means rerunning with an update flag.

Best for: Teams automating critical user journeys such as sign-up, checkout and login. Pair it with a platform like Chromatic for visual review across real browsers.

Cypress is an open-source testing tool that runs inside the browser, with time-travel debugging that shows the app at each step of a test. It supports both E2E and component testing.

Visual testing: Cypress has no built-in visual comparison and relies on plugins or a platform such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools. Cypress Cloud, its paid service, adds recording, parallelization, flake detection and add-ons for accessibility and UI coverage.

Best for: Frontend developers who want fast feedback and an interactive test runner while they build.

Storybook is the open source workshop for building UI components in isolation. Each story captures one component state, and those stories double as test cases.

Testing features: Interaction tests with play functions and Testing Library, accessibility checks with the a11y addon, and a Vitest integration that runs stories as component tests.

Visual testing: Storybook doesn't diff screenshots on its own. Chromatic, built by the same team, is its visual testing and review layer.

Best for: Design system and component library teams, and any team practicing component-driven development.

Vitest Browser Mode runs component tests in a real browser instead of a simulated DOM, using Playwright or WebdriverIO under the hood.

Visual testing: Vitest's toMatchScreenshot assertion stores screenshots in the repository. For large projects, this can bloat the repo and baselines drift between machines. Chromatic can take Vitest Browser Mode tests with no code changes and run them in a stable cloud environment instead.

Best for: Teams already writing Vitest tests who want them to check how the UI looks without adopting a new framework.

Start from the problem your team is trying to fix:

"Shared component changes keep breaking other teams' screens." Test at the component level with Storybook or Vitest Browser Mode and Chromatic, so the change shows up on the pull request that caused it.

"Our Playwright tests pass, but the layout still breaks in Safari." Functional assertions don't check appearance. Add a visual testing platform that renders in Safari, such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools.

"We turned off screenshot tests because of false alarms." Look for deterministic component states and flake controls such as Chromatic's SteadySnap, or AI matching such as Applitools.

"Designers find UI bugs after release." Choose a platform with a review and approval workflow, and make sign-off a required pull request check.

"AI coding agents keep inventing components." Give agents access to your real component library, for example through Chromatic's hosted MCP, and run every agent-built change through the same UI tests as human code.

"We have no budget." Use Playwright or Vitest screenshot assertions, self-host BackstopJS, or start on the free tiers of Chromatic or Percy.

A practical, well-covered setup for a React web app looks like this:

Unit tests in Vitest for logic and utilities.

Storybook stories or Vitest Browser Mode tests for every meaningful component state, with interaction tests on key components.

Chromatic running visual, interaction and accessibility tests on those components in Chrome, Firefox, Safari and Edge on every pull request, with designers assigned as reviewers.

A thin layer of Playwright E2E tests for sign-up, checkout and other critical journeys, also captured in Chromatic so page-level visual changes go through the same review.

This keeps most UI coverage in fast, deterministic component tests, while E2E tests confirm the journeys that matter most still work end to end.

Playwright is the best free tool for E2E UI testing, and Storybook is the best free tool for testing component states. For visual testing, For visual testing, Chromatic and Percy both offer free tiers of around 5,000 snapshots or screenshots per month, and BackstopJS is free to self-host.

UI testing is the broad practice of checking that an interface works for users, including functional, visual and accessibility checks. Visual testing is one part of it: comparing rendered UI against approved baselines to catch unintended changes in layout, color, typography and spacing.

No. Chromatic was built by the Storybook team, but it also runs visual tests from Vitest Browser Mode, Playwright and Cypress tests, and from React Native Storybook on iOS and Android simulators.

Playwright's toHaveScreenshot covers basic visual checks, but baselines live in your repo, rendering can differ between machines, and there's no review interface for designers. Teams with several reviewers or many browsers usually add a platform on top.

Most teams use both. Component tests cover individual UI states quickly and reliably, while a smaller set of E2E tests confirms that critical user journeys work across the whole app.

#10559. Nvidia proves it’s too big to fail

Good morning. Today I’m deciding whether the final Avengers: Doomsday trailer passes the “people just standing around” test, trying the new Material 3 Expressive Claude app on my Android devices, and stocking up on discounted XTEINK e-readers for Christmas gifts.

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

Eleven days after leaving Nvidia, Jensen Huang is back as president, CEO, and a board director. The board voted unanimously to reinstate him on Monday, saying it was satisfied with his candor and ability to lead following a series of meetings. It also said input from senior levels of the US government left it confident that his reinstatement poses no national security risk.

I didn’t see this coming. Huang was dishonest to the public and to his board. He admitted to having affairs not only with an alleged Chinese spy, but also with an employee who directly reported to him. He is accused of facilitating the sale of Nvidia AI chips to companies like Megaspeed that knowingly purchased them with the intention of smuggling them into China, a contravention of US trade restrictions.

As recently as November 17th, the board chair, Harvey Jones, reiterated that the company had no intention of bringing back Huang as CEO. But things changed quickly on Sunday as President Trump reportedly reassured the board that Huang’s reinstatement posed no security risk to US intelligence, something that the new Democratic-led Congress will be sure to test via repeated subpoenas in the new year.

As Huang walked into the atrium at Nvidia’s headquarters, staff gathered around to cheer his return, and he addressed the crowd by saying he would be a “better CEO the second time around,” as if 11 days of unemployment had given him sufficient time to evaluate his past behavior.

Nvidia’s stock was up over 30% on Monday, rebounding from a disastrous end to last week that wiped out $4 trillion in Nasdaq value. It’s hard not to see Huang’s return as a direct response to this short-term disruption, which also hinted at a much more serious correction in the stock market. Nvidia’s stock had already fallen 24% between the day Huang resigned and Friday’s crunch, and with looming regulation from Congress in 2027, a rudderless AI industry could have been disastrous for the US economy writ large.

Though things may appear to be back to normal, or as normal as possible given the insanity of the past couple of weeks, Huang has returned to a very different trade environment. While he was away, Nvidia’s board formed a National Security and Export Control Committee to investigate whether the company’s actions violated any US embargoes. And as part of Huang’s return, Nvidia has committed to hiring its first chief compliance officer, an executive who will ostensibly not report directly to the CEO.

I was pretty gobsmacked when I saw the news of Huang’s return come across the wire at 9am ET yesterday. I’m still wrapping my head around it. Is Nvidia too big to fail, or just a symptom of a larger interdependency between the US executive branch and Big Tech? Huang promises to be a better leader this time around, but to whom: his employees, his customers, or his shareholders? Because he appears to be a very different person to each of those groups.

Google’s latest mid-sized sedan AI model is taking the world by storm. Gemini 4 Neon is technically the middle sibling in Google’s lineup, with Carbon at the top and Hydrogen at the bottom, but everyone seems to agree it absolutely cooks.

If you’ve spent any time on X over the past few days (I hate myself for writing that), you’ll likely have seen an uptick in fervor over Google’s latest model, which seems to outperform Claude Opus 5.5, and even Gemini 4 Carbon itself, in many benchmarks and real-world tests. One thing I’ve read again and again about Neon is how adept it is at writing. I’m of two minds about this: the better an AI gets at doing my job, the less necessary I am at said job. On the other hand, I’m so tired of obvious AI slop that a model that actually writes well sounds like a relief. At least when I’m unemployed, I won’t be yelling at the screen all day about why the robot yet again used negative parallelism.

That’s an incredible number in any industry, but a truly mind-blowing one in the video game world. Rockstar also released its first patch for the game on Monday to fix a modest number of bugs with the launch code.

Its Loop Transformer reuses a block of layers to do more computation without adding parameters. The company is pitching applications beyond software, including games, visual work, and CAD. The 750-million-parameter model reportedly performs at the level of a 2-3 trillion parameter non-looping equivalent, but we’re still waiting on a broader release for real-world testing.

#10556. Build Live Video Chat With WebRTC: From First Call to Production Debugging

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Adding real-time video to a web app used to mean plugins, proprietary SDKs and a lot of money. Today every modern browser ships with WebRTC, a set of APIs that lets two browsers exchange audio, video and data directly. The catch is that WebRTC gives you building blocks, not a finished feature. This guide walks through those blocks in the order you actually need them, with the exact API calls you need, and then covers the production issues that tutorials usually skip.

A browser-to-browser call has four moving parts:

Start with the local preview. Browsers only allow camera access on HTTPS or localhost, so serve your page accordingly.

1.     Request a stream with navigator.mediaDevices.getUserMedia(), passing video constraints (an ideal width of 1280 and height of 720 is a sensible default) and audio constraints with echoCancellation and noiseSuppression enabled.

2.     Assign the returned MediaStream to the srcObject property of your local video element.

3.     Keep a reference to the stream, because you will add its tracks to the peer connection later.

Set the muted and playsinline attributes on the local video element. Without muted users hear themselves, and without playsinline iOS Safari opens the video full screen.

The peers need a way to find each other. A tiny Node.js server using the ws package is enough for development. Its whole job is to relay messages between the peers in a room:

1.     Create a WebSocket server (for example with the ws package) listening on its own port.

2.     Keep a map of room names to the set of sockets in each room.

3.     When a client sends a join message, store the room name on the socket and add it to that room's set.

4.     For every other message type, forward the raw message to the other sockets in the same room, and never echo it back to the sender.

5.     On disconnect, remove the socket from its room so stale peers do not receive messages.

In production you would add authentication, limit room size to two for one-to-one calls, and validate message types, but the shape stays the same.

Both sides create an RTCPeerConnection, add their local tracks and listen for the remote ones:

1.     Create a new RTCPeerConnection with an iceServers list that contains at least one STUN server.

2.     Loop over the local stream's tracks and call addTrack() for each one.

3.     In the ontrack handler, attach the first remote stream to your remote video element.

4.     In the onicecandidate handler, send every new candidate to the other peer through your signalling channel as a candidate message.

One peer creates an offer, the other answers, and both apply each other's descriptions. Candidates arrive asynchronously in the background.

1.     The caller runs createOffer(), applies it with setLocalDescription() and sends the description as an offer message.

2.     The callee applies the offer with setRemoteDescription(), runs createAnswer(), applies it locally and sends it back as an answer.

3.     The caller applies the answer with setRemoteDescription().

4.     Both sides pass every incoming candidate message to addIceCandidate().

If both peers might start a call at the same time, look up the "perfect negotiation" pattern on MDN. It assigns one side as polite and resolves offer collisions cleanly instead of leaving both peers stuck.

On a home network, STUN alone often works. On corporate Wi-Fi, mobile carriers and symmetric NATs it frequently does not, and the call silently fails to connect. A TURN server relays media when a direct path is impossible. The open-source coturn server is the usual choice.

In practice, your iceServers list should contain your own STUN URL plus a TURN URL with a username and credential, so the browser can fall back to the relay automatically when direct connectivity fails.

Generate short-lived TURN credentials on your backend rather than hard-coding them, and budget for bandwidth: relayed calls pass every frame through your server.

Users deny camera access, unplug headsets and switch from Wi-Fi to 4G mid-call. Catch NotAllowedError and show a clear explanation, listen for devicechange events, and use RTCRtpSender.replaceTrack() to swap cameras without renegotiating the whole call.

Listen to pc.onconnectionstatechange. When the state becomes disconnected, wait a few seconds before giving up, because it often recovers. On failed, call pc.restartIce() to try new network paths.

Use RTCRtpSender.setParameters() to cap the bitrate on weak connections, and read pc.getStats() to monitor packet loss and round-trip time. Showing a small "poor connection" indicator does more for perceived quality than any codec tweak.

Mesh calls, where every participant connects to every other, work for two or three people. Beyond that, upload bandwidth explodes. Group calls need a Selective Forwarding Unit (SFU) such as mediasoup, LiveKit or Janus, which receives each stream once and forwards it to the others.

Tutorials end at a working demo, but the fastest way to understand what production WebRTC looks like is to watch a real call from the inside. Chrome ships an internal diagnostics page that shows every peer connection the browser opens, and it works on any site that uses WebRTC.

1.     Open chrome://webrtc-internals in one tab and keep it open.

2.     In another tab, start a call on your own demo and find its RTCPeerConnection entry.

3.     Expand that entry and look at the ICE candidate pairs: you will see whether the call went direct (host or srflx) or through a TURN relay (relay).

4.     Open the stats graphs for the outbound video stream and watch the bitrate, frame rate and resolution adapt when you throttle the network in DevTools.

5.     Check the negotiated codec and how quickly the connection recovers when you switch Wi-Fi off and back on.

Once you can read your own call, repeat the exercise on a production service. Any browser-based product works, whether a meeting tool or a live video chat that pairs two visitors, because webrtc-internals shows the same data for every site. Expect differences from your demo: more candidate pairs, a relay candidate in the list and a bitrate that adapts faster. Seeing them makes the earlier sections concrete, from why TURN matters to how bandwidth estimation reacts to packet loss.

The one-to-one call above is the core of many products, from telehealth consultations and tutoring to customer support. The engineering challenges grow with the use case: random matching needs queueing and presence on the signalling server, public platforms need moderation and reporting, and global audiences need TURN servers in several regions.

Start with the minimal version, get it working across Chrome, Firefox and Safari on both desktop and mobile, and add TURN before real users arrive. Once the call is reliable, every other feature becomes a much easier problem.

#10554. The Moto G Stylus (2025) at $200 is great only if a pen is a must

Okay, so here’s the deal (literally): last year’s Moto G Stylus (2025) is on sale right now on Amazon for just $199, and if you desperately need a digital writing tool with your phone, it’s a good buy.

That is the short answer; the long answer is a little more complicated, as due to some issues with the 2026 model, you are missing out on exactly…nothing. Yes, at under $200, you are getting essentially the same phone because Motorola decided that, due to RAM and storage shortages and price hikes, the newer model would forgo any major upgrades. The 2026 model starts at $499, so if you had your eye on it, yeah, it’s a silly way to waste $300 in our opinion.

It has the same Snapdragon 6 Gen 3 processor, 8GB RAM, and 128GB storage. There’s a 120Hz display too and the good news is you get a microSD card slot to expand the storage if you desperately need it. Other notable features include a 5,000mAh battery, 68W wired charging, 15W wireless charging, a 50MP camera setup, and, as of writing, it should be due for an Android 16 upgrade.

The star of the show of the Moto G Stylus (2025) is the stylus. It boils down to this: if you want a phone that isn’t a Galaxy Ultra, then this is the only other line that makes sense for document signers.

However, there are better deals out there. I think the Galaxy A56, at just $299, is an incredible offer right now and way more deserving of your hard-earned cash. Even so, if you want clean software or that stylus, the Moto G Stylus (2025) at $199 is at least an option especially for a family member or a backup to your existing phone.

#10550. New OpenAI and Anthropic models sound alarms on safety and control, report

Frontier AI models that have yet to be released by OpenAI and Anthropic are reportedly reaching the point where employees believe they cannot be safely released or controlled, employees reportedly say.

Axios cites “more than two dozen” employees inside of OpenAI and Anthropic who are sounding warnings around unreleased models from both companies.

While both OpenAI’s GPT and Anthropic’s Claude public models are increasingly powerful, these employees say that new models behind the scenes are starting to beat out the technical solutions for controlling them. This has led to debates between those who think they’ll be able to fix these problems, and others who believe companies should stop training new models altogether due to the risks.

If we continue trying to make models much smarter, such that they’re actually smarter than us … they will in fact be able to trick us successfully. And once you get to that point, many of the schemes that we’ve had at work up until now sort of go out the window.

The report goes out to point out that some researchers feel that executives at major AI companies have become “out of touch” with the severity of safety risks, though Axios was told that OpenAI is adopting new safety practices while Anthropic said that “Keeping a model internal doesn’t make it safe,” adding that “we may slow down or hold back even when it costs us.”

Prosecutors have found recordings of meetings and conversations following the investigation into an OpenAI employee arrested for bugging the company's executive conference room.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Last month, a former OpenAI employee with ties to Saudi Arabia was arrested for bugging conference rooms in the company. Today, Bloomberg reports that prosecutors in the case uncovered hundreds of recordings, dozens of which were meetings and conversations dating as far back as September. Prosecutors believe some recordings may contain sensitive information that can be regarded as trade secrets, the dispersion of which could be a threat to US national security.

In October, we covered how Abdulrahman Alfozan, a former OpenAI employee, was arrested for installing a covert listening device behind the company's executive conference room television using a Seeed Xiao ESP32-S3. The device was discovered days later, with investigations linking it to Alfozan and subsequently leading to his arrest and removal from the company.

According to this latest report, prosecutors urged a federal judge to deny Alfozan’s request to be released on bail while the investigation is ongoing, fearing that Alfozan might flee before trial “given his relatively weak ties to the US and the considerable […] connections he has in his native Saudi Arabia.”

Prosecutors have stated, “Thus far, the government has located hundreds of audio files on the defendant’s personal computer, including dozens of recordings of meetings or conversations that occurred at OpenAI throughout September 2026, many of which involve discussions of what may be trade secrets,” as reported by Bloomberg. The court filing also says that Alfozan kept audio files and AI-generated transcripts on his computer, with some recordings uploaded to Dropbox, “meticulously categorized by date and renamed according to the content of the recording.” Prosecutors also found a collections of images of a "strategic partnership" document marked as confidential on Alfozan's confiscated phone.

The filing also states, “At the same time as he was secretly recording, cataloging, and transcribing dozens of meetings concerning (among other things) OpenAI’s partnerships and capabilities, the defendant was in frequent communication with various people affiliated with the Saudi government.” However, Alfozan denies stealing trade secrets from OpenAI for Saudi Arabia, with his lawyer arguing that Alfozan “has no special or covert ties to the Saudi government,” in a separate court filing on Friday.

According to the October 27 indictment, he is charged with interception of oral communications, not theft of trade secrets or economic espionage, as with sharing trade secrets with another government. Alfozan requested to set his bond at $1 million, but this could be denied at the hearing set for Tuesday; additional charges could also follow if the investigation finds out what Alfozan is doing, or plans to do, with the recordings and to whom he would send them.

When shopping for a graphics card, consider the following.

🔎 What resolution, settings do you want to play at? A low-end budget graphics card can play games at 1080p in medium settings, but if you want to play at ultra settings, you'll need to get at least a mid-range card. As you move up the stack of cards, you can play at 2K resolution and higher settings or, with the priciest cards, 4K.

🔎 How many FPS do you consider smooth? Most people consider 30 fps the bare minimum for playability and 60 fps decent. However, if you want less lag for eSports gaming, you'll want to be able to go to over 100 fps at reasonable settings. See our GPU benchmark hierarchy to find out how each GPU fares.

🔎 Do you have enough power? Make sure that you have enough capacity from your power supply to support the card. If you're not sure, use a tool such as Newegg's power supply calculator to see how much you need. If your power supply can't handle the card, either get a new PSU or a different card.

🔎 Will it fit in your case? If you've got a small PC case, make sure you check the length of the card against the case's clearance numbers.

#10541. Building Email Integrations: APIs, Webhooks, Automation, and Deliverability

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Email remains an important communication channel for web applications. From account verification and password resets to product announcements and customer notifications, many applications depend on email to keep users informed.

For developers, however, implementing email functionality involves more than connecting a service and sending a message. A reliable email system needs to handle authentication, dynamic content, delivery failures, user preferences, event tracking, and integrations with other application components.

Using an email service provider (ESP) can simplify this process. Most modern providers offer REST APIs, SMTP relays, templates, automation features, and reporting tools that developers can integrate into existing applications.

This guide explores the technical considerations involved in building email integrations, including API-based sending, webhook handling, automation workflows, and deliverability.

There are two common ways to connect an application to an email delivery service: SMTP and REST APIs.

SMTP (Simple Mail Transfer Protocol) is a standard protocol used to transfer email messages between servers. Developers can connect an application to an SMTP server using credentials and configuration settings.

REST APIs provide another approach. Instead of communicating through an email server protocol, an application sends HTTP requests to an endpoint. The provider processes the request and returns a response.

Both methods can support transactional and application-generated emails, but their implementation differs.

An application already supports SMTP configuration.

Developers want a relatively straightforward integration.

Existing frameworks or libraries provide built-in SMTP support.

An application needs programmatic control over email operations.

Developers want structured responses and error handling.

Email functionality needs to integrate with other application services.

The system requires event tracking or additional API capabilities.

The right approach depends on the application's architecture, expected email volume, and integration requirements.

An API integration allows an application to trigger emails in response to specific events.

For example, an ecommerce application might send an order confirmation after a successful payment. A SaaS application could send an account verification message after registration.

A typical integration follows this process:

The backend prepares the recipient and message data.

The backend authenticates with the email provider.

The application records the response and handles any errors.

Consider a Node.js application that needs to trigger an email campaign through an API.

Some platforms, including Mailmodo, provide REST API functionality for triggering campaigns and integrating external applications with email workflows.

The following example demonstrates the general structure of an HTTP request using a campaign-triggering endpoint:

This example uses the documented Mailmodo trigger endpoint and request structure. The campaign ID and API key must be replaced with the values from the relevant account.

Notice that the API key is loaded from an environment variable rather than written directly into the source code.

This is an important security practice. Credentials should never be exposed in frontend JavaScript, public repositories, or client-side application bundles.

Not every email provider offers the same integration capabilities. Developers should evaluate providers based on their API documentation, authentication methods, supported endpoints, event notifications, and integration flexibility.

For example, Elastic Email provides a REST API that developers can use to send emails and manage different email operations from an application.

Its API uses HTTPS requests and API-key authentication. Developers can use the available endpoints to send messages, manage campaigns, retrieve information, and configure webhook notifications.

When evaluating an email API, consider these technical requirements:

Authentication: Check how API keys are generated, stored, restricted, and rotated.

Documentation: Look for clear endpoint descriptions, request examples, response formats, and error codes.

SDK availability: Official libraries can reduce the amount of integration code required.

Permission management: Separate credentials for sending emails and accessing account information can reduce unnecessary exposure.

API limitations: Review connection limits, request timeouts, rate limits, and account-level restrictions before deploying an integration.

A provider with extensive features is not necessarily the best option for every project. The important consideration is whether its API supports the application's actual requirements.

Sending an email is only one part of the communication process. Applications may also need to know what happens after a message is submitted.

A webhook allows an external service to send an HTTP notification to an application when a particular event occurs.

Depending on the provider, events may include:

For example, an application could receive a delivery-failure notification and update the corresponding email record in its database.

A basic Express.js webhook endpoint might look like this:

This is a simplified example rather than a complete provider-specific webhook implementation. A production endpoint should validate the provider's signature or other authentication mechanism and follow its documented payload structure.

It should also handle duplicate events safely. Webhook providers may retry notifications when an application does not respond successfully.

An idempotent event handler prevents the same notification from creating duplicate database updates or triggering repeated actions.

Email automation connects application events with predefined communication sequences.

Instead of manually sending every message, developers can create workflows that respond to user activity.

Consider a SaaS application with the following registration process:

If the user does not complete onboarding, a follow-up message may be triggered.

A simplified workflow can be represented as:

In a production application, email delivery should generally be handled through a background job queue rather than blocking the registration request.

A queue also makes it easier to retry temporary failures, manage sending volume, and prevent email delays from affecting the main application.

Developers should consider tools such as Redis-backed queues or framework-specific job processors when building larger systems.

Automation platforms can also help manage more complex journeys. For instance, a developer can connect application events to an external email workflow instead of implementing every communication sequence independently.

The key is to keep business logic in the application while using the email service to manage message delivery and relevant campaign operations.

A successful API response does not necessarily mean that an email has reached the recipient's inbox.

Email deliverability depends on several technical factors, including domain authentication, sender reputation, message quality, and recipient engagement.

Developers should pay attention to the following configurations.

Sender Policy Framework identifies the mail servers authorized to send messages on behalf of a domain.

A correctly configured SPF record helps receiving servers evaluate whether an email originates from an authorized source.

DomainKeys Identified Mail adds a cryptographic signature to outgoing messages. Receiving servers can use the corresponding public key in DNS to verify the signature.

Domain-based Message Authentication, Reporting, and Conformance builds on SPF and DKIM. It allows domain owners to publish policies for handling messages that fail authentication checks and receive reports about authentication activity.

These three mechanisms should be configured carefully, especially when an application sends emails through multiple providers.

Email systems generate useful operational data. However, developers should avoid treating every available metric as an accurate measure of user engagement.

More reliable application-level signals can include:

These events can be connected with email campaign data to understand how communication affects application activity.

Developers should also maintain a clear separation between email delivery records and user account records. This makes it easier to investigate delivery issues without unnecessarily modifying customer data.

Before deploying an email integration, developers should review the following areas:

API security: Store credentials in environment variables or a dedicated secrets manager.

Domain authentication: Configure SPF, DKIM, and DMARC.

Error handling: Account for failed requests, invalid recipients, and temporary provider errors.

Background processing: Use queues for asynchronous email delivery.

Webhook validation: Verify incoming notifications and handle duplicate events.

Monitoring: Track delivery failures, bounce rates, and application-level outcomes.

Testing: Test email templates across different clients and devices before production deployment.

These practices help create an email system that is easier to maintain, troubleshoot, and scale.

Email integration is an important part of modern application development, but a reliable implementation requires more than connecting an SMTP server or calling an API endpoint.

Developers need to consider authentication, asynchronous processing, webhook security, automation logic, domain configuration, and event monitoring.

REST APIs are useful for applications that need programmatic control over email operations, while SMTP remains a practical option for systems that already support standard mail-server connections. Webhooks add another layer by allowing applications to respond to delivery events and update their internal records.

By choosing an integration method that fits the application architecture and following sound security and deliverability practices, developers can build email functionality that supports both immediate communication needs and long-term application growth.

#10530. Announcing Long-Context Training on TPUs with MaxText Context Parallelism

Long-context training — including pretraining and post-training, has become a foundation of frontier AI models. Longer native sequence lengths give large language models (LLMs) the working memory to read a whole code repository or ingest hours of video, and reason through multi-step agentic tasks. In three years, context windows have grown from 128K–200K tokens to 1M–2M, and models trained toward 10M tokens are arriving. Training at those lengths, not just serving them, is where the system limits show up.

In particular, efficient training at multi-million-token sequence lengths runs into the following bottlenecks:

Today, we are excited to announce long-context training support in MaxText, Google Cloud's open-source JAX training framework. MaxText enables this by supporting four context-parallel strategies on Cloud TPU: All-gather, A2A (DeepSpeed Ulysses), P2P (Ring Attention), and the A2A + P2P hybrid (Unified Sequence Parallelism, or USP), combined with load balancing and sequence packing. All four use the Tokamax Splash Attention kernel. Combined with the 3D inter-chip interconnect (ICI) ( supported on TPUs such as TPU 5p or TPU 7 (Ironwood) ), this lets one sequence span an entire slice while communication overlaps compute and memory per chip stays flat. In our benchmarks, Llama 3.1 8B (25 head dim, 16 query heads) demonstrates exceptional long-context efficiency, achieving 70% Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) on TPU 5p for sequence lengths up to 4 million tokens. Across both TPU 5p and TPU 7, context can be seamlessly extended to 14 million tokens—with TPU 7 consistently maintaining 50% MFU across all sequence scales below 6 million tokens.

This functionality is available today in MaxText v0.2.4(doc).

Context parallelism splits a long sequence across chips, so no chip holds the activations of the whole sequence. Context parallelism can be combined with data and tensor parallelism, and the smallest context-parallel size that fits your sequence in HBM is usually the right one. This also holds for sparser attention variants which cut attention compute but not the activations of every other layer.

Attention is the one global operation where every token needs the keys and values of every preceding token, so each context parallel strategy has to bring those keys and values back together. Each makes a different tradeoff between memory (how much KV a chip holds), communication (how much moves per layer, and whether it overlaps compute), and model shape (how many attention heads to split).

All-gather: Each chip keeps its chunk of the sequence and, at every layer, gathers the keys and values from every other chip. It is the simplest option. However, because causal masking causes later tokens to attend to larger prefixes, All-gather requires balancing compute load across devices.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

All-to-All (Ulysses): Gathering keys and values makes every chip hold the whole sequence, so attention memory never shrinks no matter how many chips you add. All-to-All changes what each chip owns instead. Attention is independent across heads but not across tokens, so the head axis is free to shard and the token axis is not. An All-to-All before attention rotates the sharding from one onto the other: each chip gives up the heads it is not responsible for and receives the tokens it was missing for the heads it keeps, ending up with the full sequence for a few heads instead of a few tokens for all heads. This is also why Ulysses does not need token load balancing. Attention then runs as ordinary single-chip attention with standard kernels, and a second All-to-All rotates the output back. KV per chip shrinks by the context-parallel factor, but you cannot split across more chips than the model has heads — and tensor parallelism shards that same dimension, so TP × CP is capped by the head count (under GQA, by the KV heads: eight for Llama 3 8B). The all-to-all itself, though, does not overlap with compute — attention cannot start on a partial sequence, so every chip waits for data from every other chip before any of them begins.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

P2P (ring attention): Ulysses' ceiling is the head count; ring attention removes it by never leaving the token axis. Keys and values never materialize in full. KV chunks travel around a ring of chips one hop at a time, neighbor to neighbor. Each chip attends its queries to the chunk it holds while the next one is in flight, and merges the partial results as it goes. A chip holds its own chunk and one visiting chunk, never the full sequence, and the communication overlaps compute. However, dividing attention computation into smaller chunks can reduce local kernel efficiency, and unlike All-to-All Ulysses, ring attention requires explicit load balancing.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

A2A + P2P (USP): A2A + P2P (USP): Neither strategy scales on its own — All-to-All stops at the head count, and a long ring accumulates hops while thinning the overlap that makes it work. Unified sequence parallelism composes them on two axes of the chip mesh, so the context-parallel degree becomes the product of the two: All-to-All along one axis, sized at or below the KV-head count, and a ring along the other to extend past it. Because each All-to-All group has already pooled its tokens, a ring step now carries a chunk larger by the All-to-All factor, and the ring is correspondingly shorter — more compute per hop to hide the transfer behind, and fewer hops to accumulate latency and causal imbalance. On Cloud TPU the two map onto two axes of the slice's chip mesh.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

These strategies cover the attention variants in today's major models, from multi-head and grouped-query attention to DeepSeek-style multi-head latent attention.

Splitting the sequence only pays off if the attention compute on each chip stays efficient. All four strategies run on Splash Attention, the block-sparse flash attention kernel from Tokamax, Google's open-source library of TPU kernels. It skips fully masked blocks and handles packed sequences without padding. P2P calls it once per ring step, on the local queries and the KV chunk that has just arrived, while the transfer for the next step runs underneath, in the forward and backward pass alike. Each chip does the same fused, block-level work it would do without context parallelism, with the communication hidden behind it.

P2P (Ring Attention) is the primary workhorse for very long-context training, whereas All-Gather is the standard baseline for most other cases where the KV can fit. These are the primary CP strategies to use in practice. We compare how far each can push context length on a fixed 64-chip TPU 5p slice with Llama 3.1 8B (256 head dim, 16 query heads) , and measure their MFUs. In ideal settings where both strategies can have large enough chunks (S/CP) and local batch size - the two major variables driving MFU on TPU 5p, we observe that P2P can outperform All-Gather almost every context length from 64K to 14M tokens, where communication becomes mostly hidden with the Ring circular transport schedule.

TPU v7x (Ironwood) takes the same recipe further. With twice the HBM per chip, P2P training of 14M tokens on 64 TPU 7 chips reached 48.7% Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) compared to 37.1% on TPU 5p. We observed that ring attention performs better than other context parallelism strategies -- e.g. Ulysses and USP –- on TPU 7 chips.

The choice of context parallelism strategy depends mostly on whether the gathered KV fits in HBM, and on how many KV heads the model has:

For all CP strategies, when you run low on memory: Offload before you recompute. MaxText can move saved activations, optimizer states, and parameters to host memory tensor by tensor. At long context lengths, these transfers are hidden behind compute, making offloading effectively free. Offload attention outputs first, followed by optimizer states, layer inputs, parameters, and finally remat as a last resort. MFU stays flat through the offload steps while all-gather falls off and eventually stops fitting. For more details, see the remat tuning guide.

Looking ahead, we see two natural extensions. The first is broader model coverage. Models that mix sliding-window and global layers, such as Gemma, would run the window layers with a short exchange between neighboring chips while the global layers use the ring. We also plan to support linear attention mechanisms such as Gated Delta Net (GDN) and Kimi Delta Attention (KDA). The second is performance, with further tuning of the Tokamax kernels and the P2P backward pass to narrow the remaining gap to all-gather at short context, and recipes by model and context length for TPU 5p and 7 chips.

Context parallelism is available today in MaxText v0.2.4 on PyPI, the corresponding Docker images and on GitHub main. Sample recipes are available here for TPU 7 and TPU 5p. Pick a strategy with context_parallel_strategy (ring, ulysses, or usp), set the context-parallel size, and keep the rest of your run the same. The sharding guide has the flags and constraints for each strategy, and the release notes link to the pull requests behind this work. We can't wait to see what you train.

Agent Anomaly Detection: Safeguarding agents across other clouds

Did the user ask for this? Securing AI agents against intent hijacking with Semantic governance policies

Check what your AI agents do before they do it, with Semantic governance policies
