#10529. How to Test WCAG 2.2 in CI: What axe-core Misses

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Your accessibility check in CI is green. axe-core ran, zero violations, and the badge in the README looks great. Your site can still fail WCAG 2.2.

That isn't a bug in axe. WCAG 2.2 became a W3C Recommendation in October 2023. It added nine success criteria and dropped one (4.1.1 Parsing), and most of the new ones can't be judged by reading the DOM. Deque said as much when it shipped the first WCAG 2.2 support in axe-core 4.5: the target-size rule would probably be the only new-criterion rule they added, because the others can't be automated without a lot of false positives.

So the rest is on you. This article covers the one criterion axe can check and three it can't (focus not obscured, dragging movements, accessible authentication), with Playwright tests you can drop into a project. I ran all of them against a small test page that fails every criterion on purpose, and against a fixed version of the same page. If you want a plain-language rundown of every change first, WCAG 2.2 vs 2.1 goes through them. Here I only care about what you can put in a pipeline.

Level A and AA are what most contracts and laws point at, so those are the ones that matter first. The AAA criteria are still worth knowing about.

That's four AA criteria with something you can script, one of which axe already handles. The rest of this article goes through those four.

The AA rule is smaller than most people expect: a pointer target should be at least 24 by 24 CSS pixels, or sit far enough from its neighbours that a 24px circle centred on it wouldn't overlap another target or that target's own circle. There are exceptions, including inline links in a sentence, and targets whose size the browser controls.

axe-core has a target-size rule for this. One catch: it isn't part of a default run. In my setup (axe-core 4.14.1 through @axe-core/playwright 4.14.0), a plain analyze() on the broken page reported nothing at all. The rule only showed up once I asked for it:

On the broken page this lists the three 16px toolbar buttons by selector. On the fixed page, with 32px buttons, it passes. If you've been running axe for years and never seen a target-size result, this is why.

Some context on how common the problem is. In the scan data behind my last SitePoint article, 34.5% of the 1,025 sites had controls below a stricter 44px threshold. That is a tougher standard than the 24px that AA asks for, so don't read it as "a third of sites fail 2.5.8". Plenty of those would pass. But it does show how many production sites ship tiny controls.

If you'd like a quick look at one page before wiring anything up, browser tools such as axe DevTools, WAVE, or a WCAG checker will show the same thing.

axe can't test it, because you have to press Tab and look at where the browser scrolled. Two details caught me out building this:

The check below samples a grid of points inside the visible part of the focused element and asks the browser what's on top at each one. If none of the points land on the element or its children, it's covered.

On my broken page it reported nine hits: links hidden behind the footer going forward ("Section 4 link", "Section 10 link", "Section 16 link") and the toolbar buttons and a few links hidden behind the header coming back. The output names the element and the key, which is what you want when you're staring at a failed build.

The fix is one CSS rule, sized to your sticky elements:

That tells the browser to leave room when it scrolls a focused element into view. With it in place the same test passes.

Be aware of what this can't see: a modal that opens over the page, or a chat widget that only appears after a delay, will not show up unless your test opens or waits for them. Adrian Roselli's piece on 2.4.11 and adversarial conformance is worth reading for the awkward cases.

Anything you can do by dragging needs a way to do it with a single click or tap, unless dragging is essential. Sortable lists, sliders, kanban cards, file reorderers: all of them.

You can't prove a drag alternative works from a script. But you can catch the obvious mistake, which is a draggable item with no button anywhere near it:

The limit is real. It only recognises the native draggable attribute. Libraries built on pointer events, such as dnd-kit, don't set it, so this test will walk right past them. If you use one of those, replace the selector with whatever your component renders, or add a test per drag component. Treat it as a tripwire for the common case, not a verdict.

The fix, in the fixed page, is a pair of "Move up" and "Move down" buttons on each item, each with an accessible name that says which item it moves.

You can't make someone solve a cognitive test to log in (remember a password, transcribe a code) unless there's an alternative or a mechanism that helps. Password managers count as that mechanism, which means the two ways sites break it are blocking paste and omitting the right autocomplete values.

This dispatches a synthetic paste event and checks whether anything called preventDefault() on it. It catches the old onpaste="return false" trick, and it catches script-based blockers too. It does not tell you whether your login has a CAPTCHA that needs an alternative, or an emailed one-time code you must retype. Those need a person.

On a pull request, a plain GitHub Actions job is enough:

Point PAGE_URL at a running preview, and run the tests against the pages that matter most: sign-in, checkout, anything with a sticky element. Don't try to crawl everything on the first pass. A test that fails for reasons nobody owns gets deleted within a month.

Four things I haven't tried to automate, and wouldn't:

Automation will keep the regressions out. It won't tell you the work is finished. A green build and a conformant site are different things, and WCAG 2.2 widened the gap between them.

The tests and both test pages are small enough to read in one sitting. Copy what's useful, change the selectors, and keep the broken page around: it's a handy way to check that a test fails when it should.

About the author: [name] builds WCAGsafe, an accessibility scanner. The study data above comes from its scans; the code in this article is independent of it and runs against any page.

#10527. Yes, the Pixel 10 is still a great buy [Video]

If you’re looking for a decent replacement for an older phone or a backup, and the Pixel 10 looks eerily similar to the Pixel 11, is it still worth it? Well, of course it is, but let’s get into why in this deep dive.

One of the best things about the Pixel 10 is that it looks almost identical to the Pixel 11; in some cases, it’ll work with the accessories for the newest phone – but check that before you try. So that means you’re not getting a majorly out-of-date design.

That doesn’t matter to most people, but it could be important to you. Pixelsnap magnetic accessories still work, and while the Pixel 11 trimmed down the camera bar to an all-glass visor, the overall footprint is virtually identical.

Using the same formula might be stale to some people, but it works, so why change it for the sake of it?

I’d argue that the best news is that this finally shows Google is confident, given its success with the Pixel 9 and 10 design. The company may make changes in the future, but it’s a fairly solid design that feels great after they settled on a physical form factor that works after years of fiddling, and it’s no secret that the goal is to make the switch from iOS to Android as “easy and painless” as possible. A familiar design goes a long way to calming the nerves of a potential switcher. The Pixel 10 is a perfect example of this philosophy. The Pixel 11 essentially proves that too.

Over a year on from release, there don’t seem to be any major or widespread reports of issues on the Pixel 10. I’d say that is a good omen, as people will often know within that timeframe if there are quality control issues and problematic hardware faults.

That said, the only problem I think you have to deal with is the base 128GB storage configuration. It’s not insignificant, but still not going to be tons of room for everything. If you take lots of photos and download loads of things locally to your device, then maybe go for the 256GB model.

One of the weirdest things about the Pixel 10 was that after the Pixel 11 launched along with all other supported handsets, it had a newer version of Android than the latest phone.

Yes, you read that correctly: the Pixel 10 had QPR1 before the Pixel 11.

The entire experience is just more of the same goodness that started with the addition of Material 3 Expressive last year. Android 17 keeps improving; the quality-of-life changes make for a really compelling experience that, while taking some cues from iOS, hasn’t gone full Liquid Glass. Google hasn’t quite kept the day-one update promise like the old days of Pixel, but you still have the cutting edge of the software. Even the Galaxy S26 series isn’t running a release this new and probably won’t for a while.

Rambler has even been added recently, so there is a function bleeding down from the Pixel 11, and there are loads of other quality-of-life changes and features that came with Android 17 QPR1 thrown in. Any bugs that might have been there, however limited, have been addressed in the year the phone has been around.

With the Pixel 10, you may miss out on some of the extra Gemini Intelligence perks or a couple of visual gimmicks like the Pro’s HiLight glow, but none that is yet groundbreaking enough to be drastically upset about.

Sure, the competition might offer a faster processor or newer silicon, but can they offer the fluidity of a system that is fully integrated into all parts of your digital life? That’s what the Pixel 10 delivers.

As I noted, the Pixel 10 has already seen multiple updates, smoothing out early thermal quirks and optimizing battery efficiency. These monthly security patches and Pixel Drops are a testament to the fact that your phone is a constantly evolving product.

With more updates due very soon, the prospect of an even more optimized experience, and many of the best bits of Google all rolled into one handset, it’s a damn good buy, especially as other OEMs are still struggling to get their own Android 17 updates out in a timely fashion. We’re several months on from the full launch, and barely anyone outside of Samsung has offered a wide rollout. Of course, all Pixel 10 devices get day-one updates, with six years of support still left on the clock.

There is one sore point on the base Pixel 10 that I think might make it hard to recommend over the Pixel 10 Pro. The main camera is the same as the Pixel 9a and the 10a on the base 10. It makes it harder to suggest if you care about camera quality.

It’s still super reliable and does take great photos, but it might not be the best in class like previous generations may have been. In fact, I’d go so far as to say that while it’s great you get a telephoto zoom lens, the lower-quality main sensor diminishes it somewhat.

Most people will be better off with the Pro because it has a lot more to work with, but the cameras are still very good on the Pixel 10 and are backed by Google’s excellent updates.

Even video quality stacks up, though I will say Google’s reliance on Video Boost as a workaround for lower-quality footage is a bit of a cop-out. However, it’s a good workaround, and the resulting footage is still excellent, even if I’m hoping for better on-device processing in future Pixel generations.

Ultimately, the camera is a prime example of the Pixel value proposition. You don’t need the absolute latest, greatest, multi-lens Pro Max Ultra setup to take amazing photos. You need a Pixel. The camera on the 10 series is still right up there with the best from Samsung, OnePlus, and others. It’s doing just as much with technically much less.

The biggest reason that many people who are looking for a new phone might want to consider at least the Pixel 10 lineup is that, with discounts, you’re getting a great deal — especially since Google bumped the entry price on the Pixel 11. Sure, bigger, better, faster, and shinier phones exist. They don’t give you the same experience, though. You’re paying for something that others can’t replicate, even at higher price tags.

With more updates due very soon, the prospect of an even more optimized experience, and many of the best bits of Google all rolled into one handset, it’s a damn good buy.

Is the Pixel 10 the best option for a phone right now? Suppose you want arguably the best software and are willing to forgo a few of the bells and whistles. In that case, it is an excellent alternative for anyone looking to save a little money without sacrificing too much. It’s a better buy than the Pixel 10a for most people, in my opinion, and you’ll be hard-pressed to find a more compelling mix of software intelligence and hardware value.

The DRAM and NAND shortages have driven SSD prices through the roof, but we've still managed to track down some of the best Black Friday SSD deals around. There are several 1TB and 2TB NVMe SSDs on sale — by far the most popular capacities — for less than $0.15 per GB from brands like MSI, Adata, Biwin, and Kingston. Normally, we're using price per GB as our north star here to make sure you're getting the best value for the drive you pick up.

All of the drives we've rounded up here are PCIe 4.0 SSDs, and they range in performance. Short of one exception for the 1TB and 2TB capacities, these are DRAM-less SSDs. Naturally, SSDs with a DRAM cache are more expensive. There are good deals on high-end drives like the Samsung 9100 Pro 2TB at 49% off, but we're focused on value options here. If you need more capacity, the T-Force G50 Evo 4TB at $399 is the best deal we've found.

A couple of the drives here are cheaper than when we reviewed them, in some cases a few years ago before the SSD pricing crisis took hold. Things are starting to turn in a positive direction with some of these discounts, though we still have a long way to go before we reach early 2025 pricing.

The Biwin Black Opal NV7400 is a PCIe 4.0 NVMe SSD with maximum read speeds of up to 7,450 MB/s, and maximum write speeds of up to 6,500 MB/s.

The Biwin Black Opal NV7400 is a PCIe 4.0 NVMe SSD with maximum read speeds of up to 7,450 MB/s, and maximum write speeds of up to 6,500 MB/s.

The headline deal is on the Biwin Black Opal NV7400, in both 1TB and 2TB capacities. It's a DRAM-less SSD, but it still boasts sequential reads/writes of up to 7,400 MB/s and 6,500 MB/s, respectively, and random reads/writes of 1,000K IOPS and 900K IOPS. It also comes with a generous five-year warranty and an endurance rating of 1PBW per TB of capacity.

We found that the drive was a solid all-around performer in our Biwin Black Opal NV7400 review. Prices on SSDs have continued to rise since we reviewed the NV7400 in January, but with current sales, the price on both the 1TB and 2TB versions of the NV7400 is still less than when we reviewed the drive.

If you're looking for the cheapest 1TB NVMe SSD on Black Friday, the Kingston NV3 has the NV7400 beat out by $8. Both the 1TB and 2TB capacities are on sale, though the 1TB discount is the sweetheart deal right now. It too is a DRAM-less drive, and it comes with lower-rated specs than the NV7400 at 5,000 MB/s and 3,000 MB/s sequential reads and writes, and an endurance rating of 320 TBW for the 1TB capacity.

The Kingston NV3 is a PCIe 4.0 NVMe SSD that sports sequential read speeds of up to 6,000 MB/s and sequential write speeds of up to 4,000 MB/s.

Kingston's budget drives like the NV3 use somewhat of a grab bag approach to components, swapping out the controller and flash for whatever's available at the time. Compared to prior generations, the NV3 has stronger constraints to keep performance relatively comparable across different internal components. However, there can still be differences, as you can read about in our Kingston NV3 review.

If you're looking for a drive that has a DRAM cache, you can save $50 on the 2TB MSI Spatium M470 Pro. The drive has 1GB of DDR4 per TB, 5,000 MB/s and 4,400 MB/s sequential read and write speeds, and 600,000 IOPS for random read/write performance. It also comes with a large 3,300 TBW endurance rating for the 2TB capacity, along with a five-year warranty.

The 2TB is actually $30 less than the list price when we reviewed it more than four years ago; check out our full MSI Spatium M470 review. Granted, PCIe 4.0 drives were more expensive four years ago than they were two years ago, but it's still a positive pricing move given how expensive SSDs have become.

The Adata Legend 860 is a 1TB PCIe 4.0 SSD with 6,000 MB/s and 5,000 MB/s sequential read/write performance and a three-year warranty.

Rounding out the deals is the Adata Legend 860 1TB, which is an older PCIe 4.0 rated for 6,000 MB/s and 5,000 MB/s sequential read/write performance. It squarely clocks in at $0.15 per GB, and it's just $10 more expensive than the NV3 for the same capacity.

It's a DRAM-less drive, instead leveraging SLC caching and HMB, but it comes with a five-year warranty and a respectable 640 TBW endurance rating for the 1TB capacity.

#10523. Lenovo Legion Go Beam leaks with Android instead of SteamOS

The variety of gaming handhelds from Lenovo is about to get larger. Leaked details suggest the Lenovo Legion Go Beam will run Android instead of SteamOS on a high-refresh display.

Lenovo is sticking with the Legion Go branding to offer something a little different to users. Instead of a SteamOS or Windows device, the Legion Go Beam is said to run Android (via Windows Latest). Specifically, Android 16.

That doesn’t limit it to Android titles alone. Android happens to make the perfect platform for cloud game streaming. Services like GeForce Now, Xbox Game Pass, and more offer a comparable experience to what a PC does for a fixed subscription, and that’s without the hardware internals something like the Legion Go 2 would need to run games natively.

The leak also notes the Lenovo Legion Go Beam will have a 7-inch 120Hz display, though there’s no information on what resolution or panel type the handheld might have. It’s likely 1080p or higher, though a lower resolution obviously will net users better performance. Battery is up in the air, too, other than a standard “long-lasting battery life” claim attributed to Lenovo.

This handheld takes a lot from the Legion Go 2 in design, as it should. It’s a comfortable device, and this shouldn’t be any different. It lacks a trackpad in favor of a traditional controller layout. It also has standard ports like one USB-C and 3.5mm headphone jack.

Pricing is a mystery. Devices like the Logitech G Cloud were such a hit because they delivered the needed performance at a good price. But those handhelds had a lower refresh rate. Still, hoping for a price tag around $350 or less isn’t such a bad thing.

In today's market, memory has become one of the most critical and expensive components in any build, and prices have only worsened thanks to the ongoing global DRAM shortage. We're keeping an eye on the volatile memory pricing to help identify any good deals that may arise.

Now, even more so than before the shortage began, price tags are fluctuating significantly from day to day, and it's becoming increasingly complex to tell when you're getting a fair deal versus overpaying for the same memory kit. We've compiled this comprehensive RAM price tracker that monitors the availability and pricing of different DDR5 and DDR4 memory kits across major U.S. retailers. Our goal is to take the guesswork out of memory shopping and help you make an informed purchasing decision.

We update this index regularly with the lowest prices available in the U.S. market for each memory kit. Our tracker covers a range of capacities and speeds across both budget and premium brands. We list the lowest price for each available memory kit, regardless of manufacturer, so you see the absolute best deals currently on offer.

The general availability of many memory kits is limited, with truly exceptional deals scarce and often selling out within seconds of appearing. High-capacity kits, particularly DDR5, frequently show the most severe stock shortages and price premiums. Therefore, timing and vigilance are especially important when shopping for memory in current times. Whether you're budget-conscious and want the best bang for your buck, or you're building a premium system and want the best RAM for gaming available, our index further below gives you the data you need to make the right purchase.

You can also check out our list of the best RAM and our RAM benchmark hierarchy to see evergreen performance data to help you make an informed decision when choosing a new GPU for your system.

Above are a few of the standout deals from the Black Friday event, which is currently taking place. For a more extensive list of Black Friday deals, head to our Black Friday RAM deals article.

DDR5 memory prices have exploded over the last year, with triple and, in some cases, quadruple increases. For instance, a conventional 32GB (2x16GB) DDR5-6000 memory kit sold for around $100 to $200 in October 2025, but the same kit now starts at $430, if it's even in stock.

Retailers are taking advantage of the DRAM shortage, just as they did with the graphics card shortage a few years ago. They've started raising DDR5 prices to align with current market conditions and sometimes bundle DDR5 memory kits with processor or motherboard purchases.

Nonetheless, these DDR5 memory kits sometimes sell for more reasonable prices. However, you have to be quick to pull the trigger, as scalpers and automated shopping bots tend to gobble up what little stock is left.

The rising prices of DDR5 memory kits have forced some consumers to stick with DDR4. However, DDR4 has also seen price hikes over the last three months, though not as drastic as DDR5. We've seen the pricing double to triple.

For reference, it was common to find 32GB (2x16GB) DDR4 memory kits selling for between $60 to $90 back in October 2025. Jump to January 2026, and the same memory kit could cost anywhere between $150 and $180, so DDR4 isn't exactly a safe haven for consumers.

We've seen more reasonably priced DDR4 memory kits on the market than DDR5. Logically, it makes little sense to build a DDR4-based system now due to processor and motherboard availability. However, if you're in desperate need of a DDR4 memory upgrade, there are viable options on the market.

#10520. Black Friday deals: Pixel 11 $692, Pixel Watch 5 even lower, Galaxy Buds 4 Pro $80 off, Dell PCs $1,000 off, more

We are heading into Day 6 of the giant Amazon Black Friday sale and while we are still tracking up to $400 off Pixel 11 devices, the most affordable model has now dropped down even lower and 45mm Pixel Watch 5 now starts from $326.
